The ESPRIT algorithm under high noise: Optimal error scaling and noisy super-resolution
Zhiyan Ding, Ethan N. Epperly, Lin Lin, Ruizhe Zhang

Abstract
Subspace-based signal processing techniques, such as the Estimation of Signal Parameters via Rotational Invariant Techniques (ESPRIT) algorithm, are popular methods for spectral estimation. These algorithms can achieve the so-called super-resolution scaling under low noise conditions, surpassing the well-known Nyquist limit. However, the performance of these algorithms under high-noise conditions is not as well understood. Existing state-of-the-art analysis indicates that ESPRIT and related algorithms can be resilient even for signals where each observation is corrupted by statistically independent, mean-zero noise of size 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathcal{O}(1) , but these analyses only show that the error ϵ \epsilon decays at a slow rate ϵ = 𝒪 ~ ( n − 1 / 2 ) \epsilon=\mathcal{\widetilde{O}}(n^{-1/2}) with respect to the cutoff frequency n n (i.e., the maximum frequency of the measurements). In this work, we prove that under certain assumptions, the ESPRIT algorithm can attain a significantly improved error scaling ϵ = 𝒪 ~ ( n − 3 / 2 ) \epsilon=\mathcal{\widetilde{O}}(n^{-3/2}) , exhibiting noisy super-resolution scaling beyond the Nyquist limit ϵ = 𝒪 ⁡ ( n − 1 ) \epsilon=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1}) given by the Nyquist-–Shannon sampling theorem. We further establish a theoretical lower bound and show that this scaling is optimal. Our analysis introduces novel matrix perturbation results, which could be of independent interest.



1
Introduction

Spectral estimation is a fundamental problem in statistical signal processing.
The goal of spectral estimation is to reconstruct fine details of a signal from noisy measurements.
Algorithms for spectral estimation have broad applications spanning image/audio processing and compression
[
GNHB08
]
, acoustics and electromagnetics
[
KV96
,
Sch86
]
, geophysics
[
SSF90
]
, inverse problems
[
Fan10
,
FSY10
]
, time series analysis
[
SM08
]
, spectrometry
[
SCGM96
]
, machine learning
[
HBH+18
,
QGR+22
]
, and quantum computing
[
Som19
,
LT22
]
.

In this paper, we will consider the following formulation of the spectral estimation problem: Consider a positive spectral measure

\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mu_{i}\delta_{f_{i}},\quad\mu_{i}\geq 0

representing the superposition of
d
point sources.
The
locations
of the sources are denoted by
f_{i}\in[0,1)
and the
intensities
are denoted by
\mu_{i}
.

We assume access to noisy measurements of the Fourier transform of the spectral measure:

g_{j}=\int^{1}_{0}\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}j\omega)\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)+E_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mu_{i}z_{i}^{j}+E_{j}\quad\text{for }j=0,\ldots,n-1.

(1.1)

Here,
j
is the frequency for each measurement and
z_{i}\coloneqq\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}f_{i})
are complex numbers on the unit circle
\mathbb{T}\coloneqq\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|=1\}
. The parameter
n
is called the
cutoff frequency
1
1

1

For some applications, the indices
j
have units of time, meaning the
f_{i}
have units of
\mathrm{time}^{-1}
.
For this reason, the
f_{i}
are sometimes referred to as “frequencies” in some applications.
This clash of terminology is unfortunate.
For this work, the term “frequency” refers to a frequency
j
of a complex exponential
\omega\mapsto\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}j\omega)
defining the measurements
Eq.

1.1
, and the quantities
f_{i}
are referred to as “locations”.
.

The goal of spectral estimation is to recover the locations
f_{i}
(or equivalently, the numbers
z_{i}=\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}f_{i})
) and the intensities
\mu_{i}
from the measurements
Eq.

1.1
.
In applications, it is typically sufficient to recover only the first
r\ll d
locations and intensities, which we refer to as the
dominant
part of the spectrum. Suppose
\mu_{i}
are in a non-increasing order.
In this setting, the measurements
g_{j}
can be decomposed as follows:

g_{j}=\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}z_{i}^{j}}_{\text{signal}\,\equiv\,\text{dominant spectrum}}+\underbrace{\sum_{i=r+1}^{d}\mu_{i}z_{i}^{j}}_{\text{bias}\,\equiv\,\text{tail spectrum}}+\underbrace{E_{j}}_{\text{measurement noise}}.

We see that
g_{j}
decomposes as a sum of the signal
\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}z_{i}^{j}
from the dominant part of the spectrum, a deterministic
bias

\sum_{i=r+1}^{d}\mu_{i}z_{i}^{j}
from the tail of the spectrum, and
measurement noise

E_{j}
.
In this work, we assume the following conditions on the locations, intensities, and measurement noise:

A.

Separation of locations.
All dominant locations are separated from each other and from non-dominant locations:

\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}\coloneqq\min_{1\leq i\leq r,1\leq i^{\prime}\leq d,i\neq i^{\prime}}|z_{i}-z_{i^{\prime}}|>0.

(1.2)

It is important to note that separation between non-dominant locations is not required.

B.

Separation of intensities.

We assume the intensities are positive and ordered

1\geq\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{r}>\mu_{r+1}\geq\mu_{r+2}\geq\cdots\mu_{d}>0\,.

We assume the cumulative intensity of non-dominant locations is bounded
2
2

2

The
1/8
factor is an arbitrary constant that we chose. We believe any constant strictly less than one (say, 0.999) will work by slightly changing the proof, but we have not checked the proof in detail with this modification.
:

\mu_{\rm tail}\coloneqq\sum_{i=r+1}^{d}\mu_{i}\leq\frac{1}{8}\cdot\mu_{r}.

(1.3)

C.

Independent, mean-zero, sub-Gaussian measurement noise.
We assume that
\{E_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are independent complex random variables with zero mean
\expect[E_{j}]=0
and sub-Gaussian tail decay. More specifically, we assume there exists
\alpha>0
such that

\mathbb{P}(|E_{j}|\geq t)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2\alpha^{2}}\right)\text{ for every }t\geq 0\text{ and every }j=0,\ldots,n-1.

(1.4)

Spectral estimation problem (with bias and noise):
Under assumptions A–C, estimate the dominant locations
\{f_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
(or, equivalently,
\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
) and intensities
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
to precision
\epsilon
.

This setup is directly motivated by the eigenvalue estimation problem on quantum computers, which has received significant attention recently
[
Som19
,
SHT22
,
DTO22
,
LT22
,
WFZ+23
,
WBC22
,
LNY23
,
SCS+23
,
DL23
]
.
We also expect that this setup could be applicable in many fields, such as communication, sensing, and audio processing.

When
\mu_{\rm tail}=0
(no bias) and
\sup_{j}\left\lvert E_{j}\right\rvert=\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)
(small noise), it is possible to estimate the locations (all of which are dominant, since we have assumed
\mu_{\rm tail}=0
) to arbitrary precision
\epsilon
as long as
n=\Omega(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1})

[
Moi15
]
. This is the
super-resolution
scaling in signal processing (see e.g.
[
Don92
,
CRT06
,
CFG13
,
Moi15
,
LLF20
,
LZGL22
]
), which is superior to the
Nyquist error scaling

\epsilon=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
given by the Nyquist–Shannon sampling theorem.
This scaling is clearly not feasible in the presence of large bias
or
measurement noise.
Even when
\mu_{\mathrm{tail}}=0
, the ability to solve the spectral estimation problem with
arbitrary
precision
\epsilon
as
n\rightarrow\infty
is not immediately obvious when the measurement noise is much larger than
\epsilon
, though we may still hope to resolve dominant locations with the Nyquist error scaling.
In support of this, Price and Song introduced a sparse Fourier transform algorithm designed for a continuous version of the spectral estimation problem
[
PS15
]
.
The Price–Song algorithm requires access to noisy estimates of the continuous Fourier transform
g(t)=\int_{0}^{1}\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}t\omega)\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
and, under the conditions
\mu_{\mathrm{tail}},\ \sup_{j}|E_{j}|=\mathcal{O}(\mu_{r})
, produces estimates of the dominant locations with error
\epsilon=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
, where
n\in\mathbb{R}
represents the maximum
t
utilized in the algorithm.
This work inspires us to adopt a generalized definition of super-resolution:

Definition 1.1
(Noisy super-resolution scaling).

An algorithm satisfies
noisy super-resolution scaling
3
3

3

Our use of the term “noisy super-resolution scaling” differs from that in
[
Moi15
]
, where the term refers to classical super-resolution scaling in the presence of small noise. We note that, in this definition, the error
\epsilon
may also depend on other parameters, which we suppress in the
o
-notation. Throughout this paper, the estimation error
\epsilon
is measured in the
matching distance
(see Eq. (
1.9
)).
if it recovers the locations up to error strictly superior to the Nyquist error scaling, i.e.,
\epsilon=o(n^{-1})
.

In this paper, we focus on the Estimation of Signal Parameters via Rotational Invariant Techniques (ESPRIT) algorithm
[
PRK86
]
. Since its introduction in 1986, the ESPRIT algorithm has proven one of the most effective methods for spectral estimation in practice.
The main question of this paper is as follows:

Question:
Can ESPRIT achieve a noisy super-resolution scaling for solving the spectral estimation problem with bias and measurement noise?

Why might we expect noisy super-resolution scaling for the ESPRIT algorithm?
First, due to noise cancellation, the cumulative impact of the noise might asymptotically decrease relative to the signal as
n
increases. Specifically, given a fixed, normalized vector
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
, the magnitude of the inner product between the signal and
\boldsymbol{v}
can be as large as
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n})
. From the central limit theorem, the typical magnitude of the inner product between the noise
\{E_{j}\}
and
\boldsymbol{v}
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
, much smaller.
By adapting the matrix perturbation bounds derived by Moitra in
[
Moi15
]
and employing matrix concentration inequalities, we prove that ESPRIT can estimate dominant locations with a
central limit error scaling

\epsilon=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1/2})
(
Theorem

1.2
). In particular, when
\mu_{\text{tail}}=0
and
\sup_{j}\left\lvert E_{j}\right\rvert=\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)
, this calculation reduce to the standard
super-resolution
scaling in
[
Moi15
]
.

Second, a Cramér–Rao bound for signal processing (see, e.g.,
[
SN89
]
) hints that noisy super-resolution scaling may be reachable: the accuracy in estimating dominant locations could potentially be as low as
\epsilon=\Omega(n^{-3/2-o(1)})
. This superior scaling can be attributed to the substantial increase in information contained in high-frequency data, which extends beyond mere noise cancellation as
n
increases. This paper includes a self-contained proof of the lower bound for the spectral estimation problem (
Theorem

1.7
), confirming that no algorithm can achieve an error rate better than
\epsilon=\Omega(n^{-3/2})
in estimating dominant locations (
Theorem

1.7
). We refer to
\epsilon=\Omega(n^{-3/2})
as the
optimal error scaling
.

The main contribution of this paper is a significant improvement over the central limit error scaling outlined in
Theorem

1.2
. We establish that ESPRIT achieves the optimal error scaling for estimating dominant locations
and
dominant intensities (
Theorem

1.4
). To our knowledge, this is the first demonstration of a noisy super-resolution scaling (in the sense of
Definition

1.1
) for spectral estimation under comparable assumptions.

Notation.

A complete summary of our notation is provided in
Section

A.1
.
We make special comments about our use of asymptotic notation.
Throughout this paper, most of our results are non-asymptotic, and we use the big-
{\cal O}
notation
f(n)=\mathcal{O}(g(n))
to indicate that there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that
f(n)\leq C\cdot g(n)
. Similarly,
f(n)=\Omega(g(n))
or
f(n)=\Theta(g(n))
imply the existence of a uniform constant
C>0
such that
f(n)\geq C\cdot g(n)
or
C^{-1}\cdot g(n)\leq f(n)\leq C\cdot g(n)
.
We augment
\mathcal{O}(B)
,
\Omega(B)
, and
\Theta(B)
with a tilde to suppress polylogarithmic factors in
B
.

1.1
ESPRIT algorithm and central limit error scaling

The ESPRIT algorithm
[
RK89
]
starts by rearranging the noisy measurements
\boldsymbol{g}=(g_{0},\ldots,g_{n-1})
into a Hankel or Toeplitz matrix. In this paper, we consider the Toeplitz version of the algorithm,

\widehat{\boldsymbol{T}}\coloneqq\Toep(\boldsymbol{g})\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}g_{0}&\overline{g_{1}}&\overline{g_{2}}&\cdots&\overline{g_{n-1}}\\
g_{1}&g_{0}&\overline{g_{1}}&\cdots&\overline{g_{n-2}}\\
g_{2}&g_{1}&g_{0}&\cdots&\overline{g_{n-3}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
g_{n-1}&g_{n-2}&g_{n-3}&\cdots&g_{0}\end{bmatrix}.

(1.5)

The ESPRIT algorithm (see
Algorithm

1
) is a subspace-type algorithm, which uses properties of the subspace spanned by the
r
dominant eigenvectors of the Hermitian Toeplitz matrix
\widehat{\boldsymbol{T}}
.
Note that the version of ESPRIT discussed here assumes that the intensities
\mu_{i}
are real and positive.
4
4

4

ESPRIT can also be utilized even when the coefficients
\{\mu_{i}\}^{d}_{i=1}
are complex numbers. In cases where
\{\mu_{i}\}^{d}_{i=1}
are not real and positive, we adapt the first step of ESPRIT by substituting the eigendecomposition with the singular value decomposition. The output of the algorithm consists of dominant locations and intensities with large
|\mu_{i}|
.

Algorithm 1

ESPRIT algorithm for spectral estimation

1:

Measurements
\boldsymbol{g}=(g_{0},\ldots,g_{n-1})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
; number of dominant locations
r

2:

Estimates
\boldsymbol{\widehat{z}}
and
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mu}}
for dominant locations and intensities

3:

(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})\leftarrow\textsc{Eig}(\Toep(\boldsymbol{g}))

\triangleright
Eigendecomposition with eigenvalues in decreasing order

4:

\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}(1:n-1,1:r)
\triangleright
First
n-1
rows and first
r
columns of
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}

5:

\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}(2:n,1:r)
\triangleright
Last
n-1
rows and first
r
columns of
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}

6:

\boldsymbol{\widehat{W}}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}

\triangleright
Apply pseudoinverse

7:

\boldsymbol{\widehat{\lambda}}\leftarrow\textsc{Eig}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}})

\triangleright
Compute eigendecomposition

8:

Reorder
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\lambda}}
so that
0\leq\mathrm{arg}(\widehat{\lambda}_{1})\leq\mathrm{arg}(\widehat{\lambda}_{2})\leq\cdots\leq\mathrm{arg}(\widehat{\lambda}_{r})<2\pi

9:

\boldsymbol{\widehat{z}}_{r}\leftarrow\exp(\mathrm{i}\mathrm{arg}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\lambda}}))

10:

\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mu}}_{r}\leftarrow\Vand_{n}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{r})^{+}\boldsymbol{g}

\triangleright
Optimal intensities by least-squares

Define the location and intensity vectors

\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}=\left(z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{r}\right)\in\mathbb{T}^{r},\quad\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{tail}}=\left(z_{r+1},\cdots,z_{d}\right)\in\mathbb{T}^{d-r}\,,

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}}=\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\cdots,\mu_{r}\right)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{r},\quad\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\rm tail}=\left(\mu_{r+1},\cdots,\mu_{d}\right)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d-r}\,,

In the limit of small bias and noise,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{T}}
is an approximation for the Toeplitz matrix of the
exact
signal:

\boldsymbol{T}=\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\cdot\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\cdot\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\,.

(1.6)

where
\dagger
indicates the conjugate transpose and the Vandermonde matrix
\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z})\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times k}
with vector
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{T}^{k}
is defined as

\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z})\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{1})&\cdots&\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{k})\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{where }~\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z)\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}1&z&z^{2}&\cdots&z^{n-1}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}.

(1.7)

The Toeplitz matrix
\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}}
can then be written

\widehat{\boldsymbol{T}}=\boldsymbol{T}+\boldsymbol{E},\quad\boldsymbol{E}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}+\boldsymbol{E}_{\mathrm{random}},\quad\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{tail}})\cdot\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{tail}})\cdot\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{tail}})^{\dagger}\,.

(1.8)

Here, the measurement noise is also rearranged into a Hermitian Toeplitz matrix
\boldsymbol{E}_{\mathrm{random}}=\Toep((E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1}))\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
according to Eq. (
1.5
).

When there is no bias or noise, the ESPRIT algorithm can reconstruct the signal exactly
[
PRK86
]
, which is briefly reviewed in
Section

1.4.1
for completeness. More generally, to quantify the error of our recovery, we employ the optimal matching distance: Given
\boldsymbol{a},\widehat{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathbb{C}^{r}
, the optimal matching distance is

\mathrm{md}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{a}},\boldsymbol{a}\right)=\min_{\pi}\max_{1\leq i\leq r}|\boldsymbol{a}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\pi(i)}|\,,

(1.9)

The minimum is taken over all permutations
\pi
on
\{1,\ldots,r\}
.

We first present the central limit error scaling of the ESPRIT algorithm.
The proof is given in
Section

2
, which uses matrix concentration inequalities (
Lemma

C.1
) to control
\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\mathrm{random}}\right\|_{2}
, matrix perturbation theory, and the bound on the singular values of Vandermonde matrices
derived in
[
Moi15
]
.

Theorem 1.2
(Central limit error scaling of ESPRIT).

Consider the spectral estimation problem under assumptions Eq. (
1.3
) and Eq. (
1.4
). Assume
n=\Omega\left(\alpha^{2}/(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{2}\mu_{r}^{2})+1/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
.
With probability
1-1/n^{2}
,

\mathrm{md}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{r},\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\mu_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{n}}\right).

(1.10)

Remark 1.3
(Classical super-resolution scaling).

We observe that the aforementioned theorem can capture the classical super-resolution scenario. Specifically, if
\mu_{\rm tail}=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
and
n=\Omega(1/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}})
, setting the noise level
\alpha=\widetilde{\Theta}(\epsilon\sqrt{n}\mu_{r})
is sufficient to guarantee
\mathrm{md}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{r},\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
. This condition corresponds to the scenario where the tail error and random noise are small, namely
\|\boldsymbol{E}\|=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
with high probability, suppressing dependence on
n,\mu_{r}
in the
\mathcal{O}
-notation.

1.2
Contribution

The main contribution of this paper is a novel analysis of the ESPRIT algorithm, which demonstrates the optimal error scaling of ESPRIT. These findings are summarized in the following theorem:

Theorem 1.4
(Optimal error scaling of ESPRIT,
informal version of
Theorem

3.1
).

Consider the spectral estimation problem under assumptions Eq. (
1.3
) and Eq. (
1.4
). Assume
\alpha>1
and

n=\Omega\left(\frac{r\alpha^{2}}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{5/3}}\right)
. With probability
1-1/n^{2}
, the location estimation of ESPRIT satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathrm{md}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{r},\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{r^{1.5}\alpha^{3}}{\mu_{r}^{3}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{1.5}n^{1.5}}\right)\,.

(1.11)

The intensity estimation of ESPRIT satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathrm{md}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{r},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{r^{2.5}\alpha^{3}}{\mu_{r}^{3}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{1.5}\sqrt{n}}\right)\,.

(1.12)

Here,
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
suppresses polylogarithmic factors in
n
,
\mu^{-1}_{r}
, and
\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1}
.
Note that the permutation in the definition of the matching distance is the same for both
Eqs.

1.11
and
1.12
.

Remark 1.5
(The assumption
\alpha>1
).

In the above theorem, we assume
\alpha>1
to simplify the form of our bounds.
Note that
Theorem

1.4
still yields results for the ESPRIT algorithm under small noise and zero noise
E_{j}\equiv 0
since
\alpha
is only required to be an
upper bound
on the sub-Gaussian rate of tail decay.
Our proof can be extended to provide sharper estimates for small values of
\alpha
.

Remark 1.6
(Intensity normalization).

We assume
\|\boldsymbol{\mu}\|_{1}=1
throughout this paper.
Without assuming this normalization, the intensity error Eq. (
1.12
) will have an extra factor of
\|\boldsymbol{\mu}\|_{1}
.

We provide the proof of Theorem
1.4
in
Section

3
. In our analysis, we use the result that
\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\mathrm{random}}\|=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha\sqrt{n})
and
|E_{j}|=\mathcal{O}(\alpha\sqrt{\log n})
with a probability at least
1-1/n^{2}
(see the detailed discussion in
Section

C.1
,
Lemma

C.1
). Moreover, it is worth noting that these same properties remain true (albeit with larger implied constants in the
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
notation) for any success probability of
1-1/n^{C}
, where
C>0
is a constant. Thus, our results hold for any desired inverse-polynomial failure probability.

To our knowledge, the above theorem is the
first
demonstration of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1.5})
error scaling—or indeed any noisy super-resolution scaling (
Definition

1.1
)—for any frequency estimation algorithm under assumptions A–C.
Moreover, as a byproduct of our
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1.5})
scaling for the locations
\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
, we also show that the intensities
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
can be recovered with an error of
\mathcal{\widetilde{O}}(n^{-0.5})
.

To complement our
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1.5})
scaling result for the ESPRIT algorithm (
Theorem

1.4
), we provide a lower bound showing that this scaling is
optimal
for any algorithm. The lower bound result is summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem 1.7
(Spectral estimation lower bound,
informal version of
Theorem

F.1
).

Let
\{g_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
be defined in Eq. (
1.1
) and let
\{E_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
be i.i.d. standard Gaussians
{\cal N}(0,1)
. Then, for any
\eta>0
, no algorithm can estimate
z_{i}
with error
\mathcal{O}(n^{-1.5-\eta})
or
\mu_{i}
with error
\mathcal{O}(n^{-0.5-\eta})
error for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
with constant success probability.

We present the proof of
Theorem

1.7
in
Appendix

F
.
This result is similar to the Cramér–Rao bound in signal processing (cf.
[
SN89
]
).
Due to the different settings, we provide a self-contained proof using the total variation distance between Gaussian distributions.

1.3
Related work

There is a long list of algorithms and theoretical results have been developed for spectral estimation problems under various assumptions: Approaches include Prony’s method
[
PGDB95
,
Sau18
]
; subspace-based methods such as ESPRIT
[
RK89
,
LLF20
,
LZGL22
,
LNY23
]
, MUSIC
[
Sch86
,
LF16
,
LZGL22
]
, and the matrix pencil method
[
HS90
,
Moi15
]
; optimization-based methods
[
CRT06
,
CFG13
,
CFG14
,
DL23
,
TBSR13
]
; sparse Fourier transform methods
[
BCG+12
,
PS15
,
JLS23
]
; Fourier interpolation
[
CKPS16
,
SSWZ23
,
MS24
]
; and tensor decomposition-based methods
[
HK15
,
CM21
]
, to name a few.
Among these works, a substantial body of research
[
LZGL22
,
LLF20
,
LRSS15
,
WY20
,
FL23
,
Moi15
,
CFG13
,
CFG14
,
TBSR13
,
PS15
,
JLS23
,
HK15
]
is dedicated to the classical “super-resolution" regime. This regime prioritizes identifying the smallest
n
required to achieve high precision, operating under the assumption of small noise. In particular, when
\mu_{\rm tail}=0
and
E_{j}=\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon))
, setting
n=\Theta(1/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}})
is enough for achieving
\epsilon
-accuracy.

Although the super-resolution regime only requires a small
n
that is independent of the precision
\epsilon
, the constraints of this regime may be unrealistic in certain real-world applications. For example, in quantum computing, energy estimation problems
[
LNY23
,
DL23
,
LT22
,
WFZ+23
,
WBC22
]
, where the goal is to estimate
f_{j}
accurately, often involve signals affected by non-dominant signals and random noise of order
\mathcal{O}(1)
with zero expectation. In this case, the classical super-resolution type assumption
n=\Theta(1/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}})
is no longer adequate.
To tackle this challenge, significant research has been done using approaches based on sparse Fourier transforms (e.g., in
[
PS15
,
JLS23
]
for the continuous setting and in
[
BCG+12
,
HIKP12a
,
HIKP12b
,
GHI+13
,
IK14
,
IKP14
,
Kap16
,
Kap17
,
KVZ19
,
NSW19
]
for the discrete setting). For example, in
[
PS15
]
, the authors propose a sparse Fourier transform algorithm that assumes access to the continuous Fourier transform
g(t)=\int_{0}^{1}\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}\omega t)\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)
. To achieve
\epsilon
-accuracy for the location estimation, the algorithm requires the cutoff frequency
n=\max|t|=\Theta(1/\epsilon)
when
\epsilon
is smaller than the spectral gap
\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}
.
The spectral estimation problem with bias and noise also appears naturally in quantum algorithms for eigenvalue estimation
[
Som19
,
SHT22
,
DTO22
,
LNY23
,
DL23
,
LT22
,
WFZ+23
,
SCS+23
,
WBC22
]
, but the cost model is different from the current setting
5
5

5

In quantum algorithms for eigenvalue estimation, the observation cost
g_{j}
typically increases linearly with
j
. Hence, to gather a dataset for
j=0,\ldots,n-1
, the cumulative cost is
\propto\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}j=\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
. Applying the ESPRIT algorithm with the current analysis
n=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2/3})
leads to a total cost of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-4/3})
. Consequently, the total cost of ESPRIT is higher than that of several referenced quantum algorithms, which use a higher cutoff frequency
n=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-1})
and non-uniform samples to achieve the so-called Heisenberg-limited scaling of the total cost,
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-1})
. This study may inspire further examination of the performance of quantum eigenvalue estimation algorithms when the cutoff frequency is set to
n=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2/3})
, a topic not yet explored in the existing literature to our knowledge.

.
We also note that the access model in this work is different from many previous works.
In this work, we assume we have access to a single noisy measurement at the equispaced frequencies
j=0,\ldots,n-1
; many other works assume either the ability to query a frequency multiple times, to reduce variance or the ability to query arbitrary real- or integer-valued frequencies
j
.

1.4
Technical overview

In this section, we present an overview of the new technical advancements that are essential in our improved analysis.
While these findings are specifically designed for the ESPRIT algorithm, we believe the underlying concepts may also apply to other subspace-based signal processing techniques.
Therefore, we anticipate that these technical results may be of independent interest.

1.4.1
Vandermonde matrix and eigenbasis

Recall that the ESPRIT algorithm reduces spectral estimation to matrix operations on the Toeplitz matrix
\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}}
of measurements, defined in
Eq.

1.5
.
In turn, we treat
\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}}
as a perturbation of a “clean” Toeplitz matrix
\boldsymbol{T}
comprising only the dominant part of the spectrum; see
Eq.

1.6
.
When ESPRIT is run using the clean Toeplitz matrix
\boldsymbol{T}
, the algorithm is exact.
Therefore, understanding the ESPRIT algorithm requires matrix perturbation theory to relate the matrices
\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}}
and
\boldsymbol{T}
.

The beating heart of the ESPRIT algorithm is the relation between two factorizations of the Toeplitz matrix
\boldsymbol{T}
, the Vandermonde decomposition (also defined above in
Eq.

1.6
)

\boldsymbol{T}=\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\cdot\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\cdot\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}

and the eigenvalue decomposition

\boldsymbol{T}=\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{Q}^{\dagger},\quad\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\diag(\lambda_{1}(\boldsymbol{T}),\ldots,\lambda_{r}(\boldsymbol{T}),\ldots,0,\ldots,0).

See
Eq.

1.7
for the definition of
\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})
.
Since
\boldsymbol{T}
is Hermitian, its eigenvalues are real, and its eigenvectors are orthonormal. As
\boldsymbol{T}
has rank
r
, it possesses
r
nonzero eigenvalues, which we arrange in decreasing order. Since
\boldsymbol{T}
has many zero eigenvalues, the eigenvalue decomposition can be expressed more compactly as:

\boldsymbol{T}=\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger},\quad\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}=\diag(\lambda_{1}(\boldsymbol{T}),\ldots,\lambda_{r}(\boldsymbol{T})),

where
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
denotes the first
r
columns of
\boldsymbol{Q}
.
We are ultimately interested in computing the Vandermonde decomposition of
\boldsymbol{T}
, as it contains the spectral information
\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}}
.
Lacking direct access to this decomposition, we instead compute the eigenvalue decomposition and use the ESPRIT algorithm to discover the spectral information.

The first of our main technical results is the following lemma relating the Vandermonde matrix
\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})
to the basis
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
of eigenvectors. The proof is deferred to
Section

B.2
.

Lemma 1.8
(Vandermonde matrix and eigenbasis).

Assume that
n=\Omega(1/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}})
.
There is a unique invertible matrix
\boldsymbol{P}_{v}
for which

\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{P}_{v}.

(1.13)

This matrix is nearly unitary in the sense that

\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right).

(1.14)

In particular,

\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\right\|_{2},\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{-1}}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1).

Here, and going forward,
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2}
denotes the
\ell_{2}\to\ell_{2}
operator norm.
This result allows us to transfer between the eigenbasis
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
and the Vandermonde matrix
\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})
by multiplying by a nearly unitary matrix
\boldsymbol{P}_{v}
.
In particular, the matrix
\boldsymbol{P}_{v}
is a near-isometry, so we do not lose much by interconverting between
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
and
\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})
.
We reference
Lemma

1.8
throughout our analysis, and the presence of this result is a crucial factor in determining the effectiveness of the ESPRIT algorithm.

Lemma

1.8
immediately implies the correctness of the ESPRIT algorithm when it runs with the clean Toeplitz matrix
\boldsymbol{T}
. More specifically, let
\boldsymbol{P}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}
. By
Lemma

1.8
, we have
(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r})_{\uparrow}=\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})_{\uparrow}\cdot\boldsymbol{P}
, and

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r})_{\downarrow}=\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})_{\downarrow}\cdot\boldsymbol{P}=\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})_{\uparrow}\cdot\diag(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\cdot\boldsymbol{P}=(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r})_{\uparrow}\cdot\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\diag(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{P}\,,

where the second step follows from the structure of the Vandermonde matrix. Then, we get that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}=(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r})_{\uparrow}^{+}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r})_{\downarrow}=(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r})_{\uparrow}^{+}\cdot(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r})_{\uparrow}\cdot\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\diag(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{P}=\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\diag(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{P}\,,

whose eigenvalues are exactly the location vector
\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}
. Therefore, the ESPRIT algorithm can recover
\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}
exactly when the input signal has no biase and noise.

1.4.2
Second-order perturbation for dominant eigenspace

The next main technical result is a perturbation expansion for the dominant eigenvectors of the noisy matrix
\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}}
in terms of the error matrices
\boldsymbol{E}=\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}+\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}
, defined in
Eq.

1.8
.
Let

\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}}=\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}^{\dagger}

be the eigenvalue decomposition of
\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}}
, and let
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}
denote the first
r
columns of the eigenmatrix
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}
.
In the existing analysis of the ESPRIT algorithm
[
LLF20
]
, a crucial step involves quantifying the “distance" between
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}
and
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
.
In our setting, application of standard matrix perturbation theory (i.e., the Davis–Kahan
\sin\theta
theorem,
Theorem

A.9
) can be used to show that there exists a unitary matrix
\boldsymbol{P}_{r}
such that

\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{P}_{r}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

(1.15)

Here, we have suppressed the dependence of the right-hand side on all parameters except for
n
; see
Lemma

2.1
for a full statement.
In order to observe the optimal
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1.5})
scaling of the ESPRIT algorithm, a more precise estimate is required.
To this end, we developed the following new lemma:

Lemma 1.9
(Second-order perturbation for dominant eigenspace,
informal version of
Lemma

4.1
).

Assume that the conditions of
Theorem

1.4
are satisfied.
Then there exists a unitary matrix
\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
such that

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}=

\displaystyle~\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}+\underbrace{\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}^{\perp}_{r}}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}^{\perp}_{r}}\right)^{k}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-1}\right)^{k+1}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}}_{\text{first order term}}+\underbrace{\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}}_{\text{second order term}}\,.

Here
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}=\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{Q}^{\dagger}_{r}
is the projector
6
6

6

Here, and going forward, all projectors are
orthogonal projectors
.
onto the column space of
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
,
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}^{\perp}_{r}}=\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}
is the projector onto the space that is orthogonal to
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
, and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}
are matrices of
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2}
norm
\mathcal{O}(1)
.

Although the result of this lemma is complicated, the main conclusion is this: Up to rotation by a unitary, the perturbed matrix of eigenvectors
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}
can be decomposed as a sum of four terms:

1.

The eigenvectors
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
of the noiseless Toeplitz matrix
\boldsymbol{T}
,

2.

A term of size
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\sqrt{n})
that is orthogonal to the matrix
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
and depends linearly on the matrix
\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}
(whose norm is
\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\sqrt{n})
with high probability),

3.

A term of size
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\sqrt{n})
that is contained in the range of the matrix
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
, and

4.

Second-order terms of size
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/n)
.

Our new result
Lemma

1.9
has the same
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\sqrt{n})
scaling as the standard result
Eq.

1.15
, but we have identified the structure of the leading order terms, which is crucial to our sharp analysis of the ESPRIT algorithm.

To prove
Lemma

1.9
, we represent the spectral projector
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}^{\dagger}
as a contour integral over the resolvent
\left(\zeta\mathbf{I}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}}\right)^{-1}
(see
Section

A.6
), expand the resolvent in a Neumann series, and explicitly compute all the terms in the result perturbation series.
This approach results in an explicit convergent series representation of
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}^{\dagger}
(
Theorem

D.7
) in terms of
Schur polynomials
(
Definition

D.1
).
From this expansion, it is relatively straightforward to prove
Lemma

1.9
.
See
Section

4
for details.

We believe that a result of the form
Lemma

1.9
should also exist for non-Hermitian Hankel and Toeplitz matrices, allowing our methods to be extended to study the error scaling of other spectral estimation algorithms.

1.4.3
Perturbation for
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}

Up to a final post-processing step, the location estimates
\widehat{z}_{i}
produced by the ESPRIT algorithm applied to the noisy data
Eq.

1.1
are the eigenvalues of the matrix
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}
.
Here,
+
denotes the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse (
Section

A.2
) and
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow},\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}
denote the first
n-1
and last
n-1
rows of
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}=\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}(1:n-1,1:r)
and
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}=\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}(2:n,1:r)
.
To show ESPRIT is effective, we need to show the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}
are close to the eigenvalues of the matrix
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}
associated with the clean Toeplitz matrix
\boldsymbol{T}
.
In general, the matrices
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}
and
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}
will be far apart.
However, the eigenvalues of a matrix are invariant under a similarity transformation
\boldsymbol{A}\mapsto\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}
.
Thus,
\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}
has the same eigenvalues as
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}
for any invertible matrix
\boldsymbol{P}
.
Thus, to prove ESPRIT achieves error
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1.5})
it is sufficient to construct an invertible matrix
\boldsymbol{P}
for which

\left\|\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\right\|_{2}\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{n^{1.5}}\right)

and
\boldsymbol{P}
is an approximate isometry
\left\|\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2},\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
.
This is exactly the result of the following theorem:

Theorem 1.10
(Eigenvectors comparison, strong estimate,
informal version of
Theorem

5.1
).

Assume that the conditions of
Theorem

1.4
are satisfied. Then, with probability
1-1/n^{2}
, there exists an invertible matrix
\boldsymbol{P}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
such that

\left\|\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\right\|_{2}=\widetilde{{\cal O}}\left(\frac{r^{1.5}\alpha^{3}}{\mu_{r}^{3}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{1.5}n^{1.5}}\right)\,.

(1.16)

Moreover,
\boldsymbol{P}
is an approximate isometry:

\left\|\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2},\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)\,.

(1.17)

Remark 1.11
(The matrix
\boldsymbol{P}
).

We emphasize that the matrix
\boldsymbol{P}
in the above theorem is not unitary.
In fact, we believe that if
\boldsymbol{P}
were restricted to be unitary, then the best possible scaling for the right-hand side of
Eq.

1.16
would be
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\sqrt{n})
. In our proof,
\boldsymbol{P}
is explicitly constructed to ensure the column space of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
is almost orthogonal to that of
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
. This helps us to ensure

\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}+\underbrace{\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm first}}_{=\mathcal{O}(n^{-0.5})}+\underbrace{\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm second}}_{=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})}+\mathcal{O}(n^{-1.5})\,,

(1.18)

where the column spaces of
\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm first}
and
\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm second}
are orthogonal to that of
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
. The detailed statement is presented in
Lemma

5.2
and
Section

E.1
.

Using this estimate, the optimal ESPRIT scaling
Theorem

1.4
follows immediately from an application of the Bauer–Fike-type result (
Lemma

2.2
), a standard perturbation result for eigenvalues.

This result cannot be proven using standard matrix perturbation theory alone, which only provides a weak estimate of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\sqrt{n})
(
Lemma

2.1
). To prove Theorem
1.10
, we rely on the novel eigenspace perturbation result
Lemma

1.9
, a careful series expansion of
\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}
, and the Toeplitz structure. Following the structure result in
Lemma

1.9
, we construct an invertible matrix
\boldsymbol{P}
satisfying Eq. (
1.18
) (for details see
Section

E.1
and
Lemma

5.2
). We then calculate the series expansion of
\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}
and plug in the formulas of
\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm first}
and
\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm second}
.
Ignoring higher-order terms, proving
Theorem

1.10
hinges on establishing:

\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)\right\|_{2}\sim n^{-1.5}\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|(\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm first})^{Q}_{\uparrow}-(\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm first})_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right\|_{2}\sim n^{-1}\,,

where
\boldsymbol{E}=\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm first}+\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm second}
. The first bound can be shown by the orthogonality between
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
and
\boldsymbol{E}
. Although the left-hand side involves the vectors with
\uparrow
and
\downarrow
, the difference caused by the missing entry is small, which ensures the bound of
\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
. The rigorous result is summarized in
Corollary

5.10
. To establish the second bound, we incorporate the observation that
(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\approx\mathrm{diag}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}^{-1})
and the structure of
\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm first}
into the expansion. Because of the Toeplitz structure of the error matrix, the main terms in the expansion are of the form

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{A}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{A}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})z^{-1}_{i}\,,

where
\boldsymbol{A}
is a Toeplitz matrix with
\boldsymbol{A}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-0.5})
and
\sup_{i,j}|\boldsymbol{A}_{i,j}|=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
. The Toeplitz structure allows us to show that these terms are of order
\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
, rather than the trivial bound of
\mathcal{O}(n^{-0.5})
. The detailed calculation is shown in the proof of
Lemmas

5.5
and
5.6
.

1.5
Organization

The rest of the paper is structured as follows. In Section
2
, we present the direct extension of the previous theoretical findings to prove
Theorem

1.2
for completeness. The formal statement and the proof of Theorem
1.4
are provided in Section
3
, under the assumption of the correctness of Lemma
4.1
and Theorem
1.10
. Section
4
is dedicated to discussing the proof of Lemma
4.1
, while in Section
5
, we provide the proof of Theorem
1.10
.

In
Appendix

A
, we define the notations and provide some preliminaries. In
Appendix

B
, we establish some properties of Vandermonde matrix.
Appendices

C
,
D
and
E
contain the technical details and proofs of
Sections

2
,
4
and
5
, respectively. In
Appendix

F
, we prove the lower bound for the spectral estimation problem.

2
Proof of the central limit error scaling

We begin by proving
Theorem

1.2
, which establishes a
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n^{-0.5}\right)
sample-accuracy tradeoff result for the ESPRIT algorithm. To our knowledge, previous analyses of Toeplitz or Hankel-based signal processing algorithms (e.g.,
[
Moi15
,
LLF20
]
) always assume the absence of non-dominant components to the spectrum (i.e.,
\mu_{\rm tail}=0
), making these results incomparable to
Theorem

1.2
.
The extension of previous analyses to the scenario of
\mu_{\rm tail}>0
is a relatively straightforward application of the Davis–Kahan
\sin\theta
theorem (see
Theorem

A.9
).
Furthermore, it is worth mentioning that even when
\mu_{\rm tail}=0
, previous analyses only established the central limit-type scaling
\epsilon=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-0.5})
.

The main proof roadmap is the same as the previous work
[
LLF20
]
with small modifications.
All technical proofs and calculations are deferred to
Appendix

C
.

The first step of this argument is to develop a quantitative estimate that relates the eigenmatrices
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
of the noise-free data
\boldsymbol{T}
to eigenmatrices
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}
of the noisy data
\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}}
:

Lemma 2.1
(Eigenvectors comparison, weak estimate).

Consider the spectral estimation problem under assumptions Eq. (
1.3
) and Eq. (
1.4
).
Assume
n=\Omega(1/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}+1/\mu_{r}^{2})
. With probability at least
1-1/n^{2}
, there exists a unitary matrix
\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\mu_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{n}}\right),

(2.1)

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{U}_{r}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\mu_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{n}}\right)\,,

(2.2)

where
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}=\boldsymbol{Q}(1:n-1,1:r)
and
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}=\boldsymbol{Q}(2:n,1:r)
.

This result is similar to existing results such as
[
Moi15
, Lem. 2.7]
and
[
LLF20
, Lems. 2, 5, & 6]
and our proof (in
Section

C.2
) is based on similar ideas.
The main ingredients in our proof of this result are Moitra’s bounds on the singular values of Vandermonde matrices (
Section

B.1
), standard results in matrix perturbation theorem (
Section

A.5
), and a comparison lemma between the Vandermonde and eigenbases (
Lemma

1.8
).

To convert the bound
Eq.

2.2
into actionable information for the ESPRIT algorithm, we use the following result:

Lemma 2.2
(From
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}
bounds to location estimation).

Assume
n=\Omega(1/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}})
and let
\boldsymbol{P}
be an invertible matrix satisfying
\left\|\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2},\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
. Then the output of the ESPRIT algorithm on the noisy signal
\{g_{j}\}_{j=0}^{n-1}
satisfies

\mathrm{md}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{r},\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})=\mathcal{O}\left(r\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}\right).

(2.3)

Furthermore, if

\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}<c\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}/2,

(2.4)

for a sufficiently small constant
c\in(0,1)
that only depends on
\boldsymbol{P}
, then

\mathrm{md}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{r},\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})=\mathcal{O}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}\right).

(2.5)

This result slightly improves Lemma 2 in
[
LLF20
]
and is proven by the combination of the Bauer–Fike theorem (
Theorem

A.11
) and the resolvent approach for eigenvalue perturbation (
Lemma

A.13
). The proof is deferred to
Section

C.3
.

Combining
Lemmas

2.1
and
2.2
with
\boldsymbol{P}=\boldsymbol{U}
immediately yields the
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-0.5})
error bound in
Theorem

1.2
.

Ultimately, the loss in this argument is that the perturbation bounds in
Lemma

2.1
are too weak.
To prove the
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1.5})
error scaling (i.e.,
Theorem

1.4
), we will employ the stronger estimate
Theorem

1.10
.
Combining this strong estimate
Theorem

1.10
with
Lemma

2.2
will prove
Theorem

1.4
.

3
Proof of the optimal error scaling

In this section, we present the formal statement of our main result (
Theorem

1.4
) and the proof.

Theorem 3.1
(Optimal error scaling of ESPRIT,
formal version of
Theorem

1.4
).

Consider the spectral estimation problem under assumptions Eq. (
1.3
) and Eq. (
1.4
). Assume
\alpha>1
and

\displaystyle n=\Omega\left(\left(\frac{r\alpha^{4/3}}{\mu_{r}^{4/3}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{2/3}}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\cdot\log(r,\alpha,1/\mu_{r},1/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}})\right)\,.

(3.1)

With probability
1-1/n^{2}
, the location estimation of ESPRIT satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathrm{md}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{r},\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})

\displaystyle={\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{3/2}\right)\frac{r\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{3/2}}\right)\,.

(3.2)

If we further assume

\displaystyle n=\Omega\left(\left(\frac{r\alpha^{4/3}}{\mu_{r}^{4/3}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{4/3}}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{5/3}}\right)\cdot\log(r,\alpha,1/\mu_{r},1/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}})\right)\,,

(3.3)

then the location estimation satisfies:

\displaystyle{\rm md}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{r},\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})={\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{3/2}\right)\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{3/2}}\right)\,,

(3.4)

and the intensity estimation of ESPRIT satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathrm{md}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{r},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})={\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{3/2}\right)\frac{r\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}\sqrt{n}}\right)\,.

(3.5)

Note that the permutation in the definition of the matching distance is the same for both
Eqs.

3.4
and
3.5
.

The high-level idea of the proof is as follows. First, the location estimation result, established in
Theorem

1.4
, is a direct consequence of the strong eigenvector comparison result introduced in
Theorem

1.10
. Specifically, after demonstrating that
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}
is approximately similar to
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}
, it becomes apparent that the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}
and
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}
are within
\mathcal{O}(n^{-1.5})
proximity to each other (see
Lemma

2.2
). Subsequently, establishing the proximity between
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{r}
and
\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}
allows us to conclude that
\Vand_{n}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{r})
closely approximates the exact Vandermonde matrix
\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})
. The intensity estimation result can then be derived through perturbation theory applied to the least-squares problem.

Proof of
Theorem

3.1
.

We prove the error scaling of the location estimation and intensity estimation of the ESPRIT algorithm below.

Location estimation.

According to
Theorem

5.1
(formal version of
Theorem

1.10
) and the assumption on
n
(
Eq.

3.1
), with probability at least
1-1/n^{2}
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}=

\displaystyle\cal{\displaystyle O}(1)\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}

(3.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cal{\displaystyle O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{3/2}\right)\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{3/2}}\right)\,,

where we use
\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\right\|_{2},\left\|\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
by
Eq.

1.17
in the first step.

Then, by
Eq.

2.3
in
Lemma

2.2
, we obtain that

\displaystyle{\rm md}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{r},\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})={\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{3/2}\right)\frac{r\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}\right)\,.

The first location estimation guarantee (Eq. (
3.2
)) is proved.

If we further assume that a stronger condition on
n
(
Eq.

3.3
) holds,
then by
Eq.

3.6
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}<c\frac{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}{2}\,,

where
c\in(0,1)
that satisfies the condition (
Eq.

2.4
) in
Lemma

2.2
. By
Eq.

2.5
, we obtain that

\displaystyle{\rm md}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{r},\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})={\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{3/2}\right)\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}\right)\,.

The second location estimation guarantee (
Eq.

3.4
) is proved.

Intensity estimation.

To show Eq. (
1.12
), we assume that the optimal permutation
\pi
is identity without loss of generality. Let
\boldsymbol{V}_{r}=\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}=\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{r})
. By Line
10
of
Algorithm

1
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mu}}_{r}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{+}\boldsymbol{g}=\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{\dagger}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}\right)^{-1}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{\dagger}\big(\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}}+\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{tail}})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\rm tail}+\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(:,1)\big)\,.

Here,
\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(:,1)
represents the first column of
\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}
.
This implies

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mu}}_{r}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{+}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}-\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\right)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{+}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{tail}})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{tail}}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{+}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(:,1)\right\|_{2}\,.

(3.7)

To bound the three terms, we first observe that when
n
is sufficiently large, i.e.,
Eq.

3.3
holds, the location estimation (
Eq.

1.11
) guarantees that
\|\boldsymbol{z}_{\rm dom}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{r}\|_{\infty}<\frac{1}{4}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}
, which implies that

\displaystyle\min_{1\leq i,j\leq r,i\neq j}|\widehat{z}_{i}-\widehat{z}_{j}|\geq\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}/2,\quad\min_{1\leq i\leq r,1\leq j\leq d,i\neq j}|\widehat{z}_{i}-z_{j}|\geq\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}/2\,.

(3.8)

For the first term in
Eq.

3.7
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{+}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}-\boldsymbol{I}_{r})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\rm dom}\right\|_{2}=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{\dagger}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}\right)^{-1}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}-\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\right)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}}\right\|_{2}

(3.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(r^{1/2}\right)\cdot\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{+}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}-\boldsymbol{I}_{r}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{+}(\boldsymbol{V}_{r}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(r^{1/2}\right)\cdot\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{+}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{V}_{r}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}\right\|_{2}\,,

where the second equality follows from
\left\|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\rm dom}\right\|_{2}={\cal O}(r^{1/2})
, and the last equality follows from
\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{+}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}=\boldsymbol{I}_{r}
by the definition of pseudoinverse.
For
\big\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{+}\big\|_{2}
, since
\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}
is a Vandermonde matrix, by
Eq.

3.8
and
Theorem

B.1
,

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{+}\right\|_{2}=\sigma_{\min}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}\right)^{-1}={\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\,.

(3.10)

Next, for
\left\|\boldsymbol{V}_{r}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}\right\|_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{V}_{r}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{V}_{r}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}\right\|_{F}^{2}=

\displaystyle~\sum_{1\leq i\leq r,0\leq j\leq n-1}|z^{j}_{i}-\widehat{z}^{j}_{i}|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{1\leq i\leq r,0\leq j\leq n-1}{\cal O}\left(j^{2}\right)\cdot|z_{i}-\widehat{z}_{i}|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{0\leq j\leq n-1}{\cal O}(j^{2})\cdot r\cdot{\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}+r^{3}\right)\frac{\alpha^{4}\log^{2}n}{\mu_{r}^{4}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{2}n^{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~{\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}+r^{3}\right)\frac{r\alpha^{4}\log^{2}n}{\mu_{r}^{4}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{2}}\right)\,,

where the second equality follows from
|z_{i}|=|\widehat{z}_{i}|=1
for any
i\in[r]
, and the third equality follows from the location estimation guarantee (
Eq.

3.4
). Hence,

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{V}_{r}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}\right\|_{2}={\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{3/2}\right)\frac{\sqrt{r}\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\,.

(3.11)

Plugging
Eqs.

3.10
and
3.11
back into Eq. (
3.9
), we obtain

\left\|\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{+}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}-\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\right)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}}\right\|_{2}={\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{3/2}\right)\frac{r\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}\sqrt{n}}\right)\,.

For the second term in
Eq.

3.7
, using
Eq.

3.8
and a similar proof as
Proposition

C.3
, we have

\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{tail}})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{tail}}\right\|_{2}\leq\sum^{d}_{j=r+1}\mu_{j}\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{j})\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\,.

Similar to
Eq.

3.10
, by
Eq.

3.8
and
Theorem

B.1
, we also have
\|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{\dagger}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r})^{-1}\|_{2}={\cal O}(\frac{1}{n})
.
Combining them together, we obtain

\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{+}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{tail}})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{tail}}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{\dagger}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r})^{-1}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{tail}})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{tail}}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\mu_{r}}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\,.

(3.12)

For the last term in
Eq.

3.7
, by the concentration inequality of sub-Gaussian vector (see e.g.,
[
JNG+19
]
),
\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(:,1)\|_{2}={\cal O}(\alpha\sqrt{n\log n})
with probability
1-1/(2n^{2})
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{+}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(:,1)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{\dagger}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}^{\dagger}_{r}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(:,1)\right\|_{2}+\left\|\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{\dagger}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}-\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(:,1)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{\dagger}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}^{\dagger}_{r}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(:,1)\right\|_{2}+{\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{3/2}\right)\frac{\sqrt{r}\alpha^{3}\log^{3/2}n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}\sqrt{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\cal{\displaystyle O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{V}^{\dagger}_{r}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(:,1)\right\|_{2}+{\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{3/2}\right)\frac{\sqrt{r}\alpha^{3}\log^{3/2}n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}\sqrt{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\cal{\displaystyle O}\left(\frac{\sqrt{r}}{n}\right)\cdot\max_{1\leq i\leq r}~\left|\boldsymbol{v}^{\dagger}_{n}(z_{i})\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(:,1)\right|+{\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{3/2}\right)\frac{\sqrt{r}\alpha^{3}\log^{3/2}n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}\sqrt{n}}\right)\,

Here, we use
Eq.

3.11
in the third step and
Eq.

3.10
in the third and fourth steps.
Notice that
\boldsymbol{v}^{\dagger}_{n}(z_{i})\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(:,1)
is sub-Gaussian with parameter
\left\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})\right\|_{2}\cdot\alpha=\alpha\sqrt{n}
. By sub-Gaussian concentration and union bound, we obtain that with probability
1-1/(2n^{2})
,

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq r}~\left|\boldsymbol{v}^{\dagger}_{n}(z_{i})\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(:,1)\right|={\cal O}(\alpha\sqrt{n\log(nr)})\,.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{r}^{+}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(:,1)\right\|_{2}=

\displaystyle~{\cal O}\left(\frac{\sqrt{r}\alpha\sqrt{\log(nr)}}{\sqrt{n}}+\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{3/2}\right)\frac{\sqrt{r}\alpha^{3}\log^{3/2}n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}\sqrt{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~{\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{3/2}\right)\frac{\sqrt{r}\alpha^{3}\log^{3/2}n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}\sqrt{n}}\right)\,.

Plugging these three terms back into
Eq.

3.7
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mu}}_{r}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}}\right\|_{2}=

\displaystyle~{\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{3/2}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{r\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}\sqrt{n}}+\frac{\sqrt{r}\alpha^{3}\log^{3/2}n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}\sqrt{n}}\right)+\frac{\mu_{r}}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~{\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{3/2}\right)\frac{r\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}\sqrt{n}}\right)\,.

where the last step follows from the assumption of
n
(
Eq.

3.3
).

The proof of the theorem is completed.
∎

4
Second-order eigenvector perturbation theory

In this section, we provide a formal statement of the second-order eigenvector perturbation result in
Lemma

1.9
. We outline the main steps involved in proving this lemma and defer technical details to
Appendix

D
. In the following part of this section, we always assume Eq. (
1.3
), Eq. (
1.4
), and
Theorem

3.1
Eq. (
3.1
) and omit these conditions later.

Lemma 4.1
(Second-order perturbation for dominant eigenspace,
formal version of
Lemma

1.9
).

There exists a unitary matrix
\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
such that

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}=

\displaystyle~\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{n}}\right)\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}\boldsymbol{\widetilde{Q}}_{1}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}+\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)\right)\boldsymbol{\widetilde{Q}}_{2},

(4.1)

where
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{Q}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{Q}}_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
are matrices of unit norm
\left\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}\right\|_{2}=1
.

Furthermore, it holds that

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}=

\displaystyle~\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}}\right)\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}\boldsymbol{\widetilde{Q}}_{1}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\boldsymbol{\widetilde{Q}}_{2}^{\prime},

(4.2)

where
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
with
\left\|\boldsymbol{\widetilde{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}=1
.

Recall that for a matrix
\boldsymbol{U}
with orthonormal columns, we notate the projectors
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{U}}=\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{U}^{\dagger}
and
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{U}^{\perp}}=\mathbf{I}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{U}}
.
We will extensively use the contour integral representation of the spectral projectors
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}^{\dagger}
and
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}
of
\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}}
and
\boldsymbol{T}
(
Section

A.6
):

Proposition 4.2
(Expansion of spectral projector).

It holds that

\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}}=\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{r}}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{T})^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{E}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{T})^{-1}\right)^{k}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\,,

where
\mathcal{C}_{r}
is a circle in the complex plane defined below in
Eq.

4.5
.

Proof of
Proposition

4.2
.

From
Lemma

A.15
, it is sufficient to prove
\|\boldsymbol{E}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{T})^{-1}\|<1
on an appropriate circle
\mathcal{C}_{r}
.
For convenience, we break the proof up into steps.

Step 1: Where are the eigenvalues?

By
Corollary

B.3
and
n>\frac{4\pi}{\Delta_{z}}+2
, we know that

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\boldsymbol{T})\leq\mu_{1}\left(n-1+\frac{2\pi}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}n\mu_{1}\,,\quad\lambda_{r}(\boldsymbol{T})\geq\mu_{r}\left(n-1-\frac{2\pi}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}n\mu_{r}\,.

(4.3)

Then, by Weyl’s perturbation theorem (
Theorem

A.7
) and
Proposition

C.2
, the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}}
satisfy

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}})\leq

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\boldsymbol{T})+\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{2}\leq\frac{3}{2}n\mu_{1}+n\mu_{\rm tail}+{\cal O}(\alpha\sqrt{n\log n})\leq\frac{27}{16}n\mu_{1}\,,

(4.4)

\displaystyle\lambda_{r}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}})\geq

\displaystyle\lambda_{r}(\boldsymbol{T})-\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}n\mu_{r}-n\mu_{\rm tail}-{\cal O}(\alpha\sqrt{n\log n})\geq\frac{5}{16}n\mu_{r}\,,

\displaystyle|\lambda_{r+1}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}})|\leq

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{2}=n\mu_{\rm tail}+{\cal O}(\alpha\sqrt{n\log n})\leq\frac{3}{16}n\mu_{r}\,,

assuming
\mu_{\rm tail}\leq\frac{1}{8}\mu_{r}
and sufficiently large
n
(Eq. (
3.1
))

Let
\mathcal{C}_{r}
be a circle in the complex plane centered at
n(\mu_{1}+\frac{1}{8}\mu_{r})
with radius
n(\mu_{1}-\frac{1}{8}\mu_{r})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{r}=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}~\Big|~\left|z-n\left(\mu_{1}+\frac{1}{8}\mu_{r}\right)\right|=n\left(\mu_{1}-\frac{1}{8}\mu_{r}\right)\right\}\,.

(4.5)

Then, by
Eqs.

4.3
and
4.4
, the following properties hold (see
Fig.

1
for an illustration):

a)

The first
r
eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{T}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{T}}
are included in
\mathcal{C}_{r}
.

b)

All remaining eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{T}
(which are zero) and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{T}}
are outside of
\mathcal{C}_{r}
.

c)

For any
z\in\mathcal{C}_{r}
and
1\leq i\leq d
,

\displaystyle|z-\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{T})|\geq\frac{1}{4}n\mu_{r}\,.

(4.6)

Thus,
\mathcal{C}_{r}
satisfies the eigenvalue conditions in
Lemma

A.15
.

\mathrm{Re}
\mathrm{Im}
n(\mu_{1}-\frac{1}{8}\mu_{r})
\frac{n\mu_{r}}{4}
2n\mu_{1}
\widehat{\lambda}_{r+1}
\widehat{\lambda}_{r+2}
\lambda_{1}
\lambda_{2}
\lambda_{3}
\cdots
\lambda_{r}
\widehat{\lambda}_{1}
\widehat{\lambda}_{2}
\widehat{\lambda}_{3}
\widehat{\lambda}_{r}
\mathcal{C}_{r}

Figure 1
:
All the non-zero eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{T}
and the first
r
eigenvalues of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{T}}
are contained in
\mathcal{C}_{r}
.

Step 2: Bound the error on the circle.

Next, we establish a uniform bound on
\left\|(\zeta-\boldsymbol{T})^{-1}\boldsymbol{E}\right\|_{2}
so that we may apply
Lemma

A.15
. Fix any
\zeta\in\mathcal{C}_{r}
.
We begin with the triangle inequality

\left\|\boldsymbol{E}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{T})^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{T})^{-1}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{T})^{-1}\right\|_{2}.

(4.7)

For the first term, we use
Lemma

C.1
to obtain the bound

\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{T})^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|(\zeta-\boldsymbol{T})^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq{\cal O}(\alpha\sqrt{n\log n})\cdot\frac{4}{n\mu_{r}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{n}}\right)\,,

(4.8)

where the second step follows from
Eq.

4.6
that

\left\|(\zeta-\boldsymbol{T})^{-1}\right\|_{2}=\max_{i\in[d]}~|\zeta-\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{T})|^{-1}\leq\frac{4}{n\mu_{r}}\,.

For the second term in
Eq.

4.7
, consider the spectral decomposition

\displaystyle(\zeta-\boldsymbol{T})^{-1}=\boldsymbol{Q}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{\Lambda})^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}^{\dagger}=\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r})^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}^{\dagger}_{r}+\zeta^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\,.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{T})^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle~\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r})^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}+|\zeta^{-1}|\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|(\zeta-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r})^{-1}\right\|_{2}+|\zeta^{-1}|\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{4}{n\mu_{r}}\cdot\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\boldsymbol{Q}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\right\|_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}\mu_{r}n}\right)+\frac{4\mu_{\rm tail}}{\mu_{r}}\,,

where the first step follows from the triangle inequality, the second step follows from
\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\|_{2}=\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\|_{2}=1
, the third step follows from
\left\|(\zeta-\boldsymbol{T})^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{4}{n\mu_{r}}
, the fourth step follows from
Proposition

C.2
and
Proposition

C.3
.

Plugging the above two displays into
Eq.

4.7
and using the assumptions on
n
and
\mu_{\rm tail}
, we obtain that

\left\|\boldsymbol{E}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{T})^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq{\cal O}\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{n}}+\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}\mu_{r}n}\right)+\frac{4\mu_{\rm tail}}{\mu_{r}}<1\,.

This bound holds for any
\zeta\in\mathcal{C}_{r}
, so we obtain the desired expansion by
Lemma

A.15
.
∎

The series expansion in
Proposition

4.2
is rather nontransparent, as the higher-order terms are expressed in terms of contour integrals.
Fortunately, these integrals can be evaluated exactly, leading to explicit formulas for all terms in the expansion in terms of Schur polynomials.
As the results are rather intricate, so we defer the statement and proof to
Section

D.1
.
We can then bound the higher-order terms in this expression, yielding the following representation:

Lemma 4.3
(Expansion of spectral projector, simplified).

It holds that

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}}=&~\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\left({\boldsymbol{T}}^{+}\right)^{k}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{E}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}})^{k-1}+(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\left({\boldsymbol{T}}^{+}\right)^{k}\right)\\
~&+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\right)\boldsymbol{G},\end{split}

(4.9)

for some matrix
\boldsymbol{G}
of norm
\left\|\boldsymbol{G}\right\|_{2}=1
.

To complete the proof of
Lemma

4.1
, we just need to transfer this result about the projectors
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}}
and
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}
to the rectangular matrices
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}
and
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
.
To do so, we use
Proposition

A.10
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.1
.

From the proof of
Lemma

2.1
(specifically,
Eq.

C.2
), we know that there exists a unitary matrix
\boldsymbol{U}_{r}
such that

\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{n}}\right)\,,

where we omit
\mu_{\rm tail}/\mu_{r}<1
.

On the other hand, notice that

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}\right\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left\|(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left\|(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(\boldsymbol{T}^{+})^{k}\right\|_{2}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\sqrt{n}\mu_{r}}\right)\cdot C^{k-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{n}}\right)\,,

where the first step follows from the triangle inequality, the second step follows from the definition of the pseudo-inverse of
\boldsymbol{T}
, the third step follows from
Eq.

D.7
, and the last step follows from
0<C<\frac{1}{2}
by
Lemma

D.8
.

Thus, we can write

\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}=\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}+\left(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}}+\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\right)\boldsymbol{R}

(4.10)

where
\boldsymbol{R}
is the remainder matrix of norm

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{R}\right\|_{2}=

\displaystyle~\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{n}}\right).

To prove the result, we just need to bound
\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{R}}\right\|_{2}
.
To do so, we multiply each side of
Eq.

4.10
with its adjoint. Since
\boldsymbol{U}_{r}
is a unitary matrix, the LHS becomes

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}^{\dagger}\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}^{\dagger}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}^{\dagger}=\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}}\,.

The RHS becomes

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}+\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}\right)^{\dagger}+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}\right)\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{R}^{\dagger}+\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}+\boldsymbol{R}\right)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}+\boldsymbol{R}\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=
