\displaystyle~\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{T}^{+})^{k}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{E}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}})^{k-1}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}(\boldsymbol{T}^{+})^{k}

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{R}^{\dagger}+\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}n}\right)\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}\,,

where
\boldsymbol{G^{\prime}}
is some matrix of norm
\|\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}\|_{2}=1
, and the last step follows from

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}+\boldsymbol{R}\right)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}+\boldsymbol{R}\right)^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~{\cal O}\left(\Big\|\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}\Big\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{R}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~{\cal O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}n}+\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{2}n^{2}}\right)={\cal O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}n}\right)

Comparing LHS and RHS with
Eq.

4.9
, we see that

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{R}^{\dagger}+\boldsymbol{R}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}=

\displaystyle~\left\|\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\right)\boldsymbol{G}-\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}n}\right)\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\right)\,.

Now, we estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{R}\right\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle~\left\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{R}^{\dagger}+\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}\right)^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{R}^{\dagger}+\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\right)\,,

where the second step follows from
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}=\boldsymbol{0}
.

Plugging this estimate into
Eq.

4.10
gives
4.1
.

For the furthermore part, fix any
k\geq 2
and consider the difference of replacing
\boldsymbol{E}
with
\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}
in
4.1
:

\displaystyle\left\|(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}-(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\left\|(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}+\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}))^{k-1}-(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail})^{k-1}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{t=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{t}\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\right\|_{2}^{t}\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\right\|_{2}^{k-1-t}\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{t=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{t}\cdot{\cal O}\left(\alpha\sqrt{n\log n}\right)^{t+1}\cdot(n\mu_{\rm tail})^{k-1-t}\cdot{\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{n\mu_{r}}\right)^{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~{\cal O}\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\mu_{r}}\right)^{k-2}\cdot{\cal O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}n}\right)\,,

where the third step follows from
Lemmas

C.1
,
C.2
and
B.3
, and the last step follows from the identity
\binom{m}{p}\leq 2^{m}
.

Summing over
k
, we get that

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}-(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}{\cal O}\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\mu_{r}}\right)^{k-2}\cdot{\cal O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~{\cal O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}n}\right)\,,

where the last step follows from
\mu_{\rm tail}<\mu_{r}/8
. Therefore, we obtain that

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}=

\displaystyle~\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{n}}\right)\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}\boldsymbol{\widetilde{Q}}_{1}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}+\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)\right)\boldsymbol{\widetilde{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail})^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{r}^{-k}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}}\right)\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}\boldsymbol{\widetilde{Q}}_{1}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\boldsymbol{\widetilde{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}\,,

where
\left\|\boldsymbol{\widetilde{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}=1
, and the last step follows from
n=\Omega(\alpha^{-2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1})
.

The proof of the lemma is then completed.
∎

5
Strong eigenvector comparison

The goal of this section is to prove the strong version of the eigenvectors comparison theorem stated below, which is the key component of obtaining the optimal convergence rate of the ESPRIT algorithm.

Theorem 5.1
(Eigenvectors comparison: strong estimation,
formal version of Theroem
1.10
).

Assume Eq. (
1.3
) and Eq. (
1.4
).
Suppose

n=\Omega\left(\left(\frac{r\alpha^{4/3}}{\mu_{r}^{4/3}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{2/3}}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\cdot\log(r,\alpha,1/\mu_{r},1/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}})\right)\,.

Then, there exists a unitary matrix
\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
and an invertible (not necessarily unitary) matrix
\boldsymbol{P}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
such that

\left\|\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}^{+}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{Q}^{+}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right\|_{2}={\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}+r^{1.5}\right)\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}\right)\,,

(5.1)

where
[\cdot]^{+}
is the pseudo-inverse. Here
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}=\boldsymbol{Q}(:n-1,:r)
is the first
n-1
rows and first
r
columns of
\boldsymbol{Q}
and
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}=\boldsymbol{Q}(1:,:r)
is the last
n-1
rows and first
r
columns of
\boldsymbol{Q}
.

For simplicity, let
\boldsymbol{E}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{E}_{2}
be the following two parts of the error matrix
\boldsymbol{E}
:

\boldsymbol{E}_{1}:=\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}=\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{tail}})\cdot\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{tail}})\cdot\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{tail}})^{\dagger},\quad\boldsymbol{E}_{2}:=\boldsymbol{E}_{\mathrm{random}}\,.

Our proof of
Theorem

5.1
consists of three steps:

•

Step 1:
Construction of the “good” aligning matrix
\boldsymbol{P}
(
Section

5.1
)

•

Step 2:
Taylor expansion with respect to the error terms (
Section

5.2
)

•

Step 3:
Error cancelation in the Taylor expansion (
Section

5.3
)

We will introduce these three steps in
Section

5.1
-
Section

5.3
and prove
Theorem

5.1
in
Section

5.4
. Most detialed calculations are deferred to
Appendix

E
.

5.1
Construction of the “good”
\boldsymbol{P}

In this step, we employ the second-order perturbation expansion of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}
in terms of the error matrices
\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}
and
\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}
(
Lemma

1.9
) to construct an invertible matrix
\boldsymbol{P}
designed to eliminate all error terms whose column space aligns with that of
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
. More specifically, as summarized in the following lemma, the matrix
\boldsymbol{P}
defined as
Eq.

5.2
is almost-identity (Property I) and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
has certain structure (Property II), which is crucial for the subsequent two steps.

Lemma 5.2
.

There exists two matrices
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times r}
satisfying the following properties:

•

\left\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}\right\|_{2}={\cal O}(1)
,
\left\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}\right\|_{2}={\cal O}(1)
.

•

Define an matrix
\boldsymbol{P}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}:=\boldsymbol{I}_{r}+{\cal O}\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{z}n}}\right)\boldsymbol{Q}^{\dagger}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}+{\cal O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\boldsymbol{Q}^{\dagger}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}\,,

(5.2)

where
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}
is the projection onto the column space of
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
. Then,
\boldsymbol{P}
is an invertible matrix and satisfies the following two properties:

–

Property I:

\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}={\cal O}\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{z}n}}\right)\,,\quad\left\|\boldsymbol{P}-\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}={\cal O}\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{z}n}}\right)\,.

(5.3)

–

Property II: There exists a unitary matrix
\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}\leq{\cal O}\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{z}n}}\right)\,,

(5.4)

and

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}+\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{n}-\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\right)\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}^{k}\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-1}\right)^{k+1}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}

(5.5)

\displaystyle+{\cal O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{z}n}\right)\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}^{\perp}_{r}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}+{\cal O}\left(\frac{\alpha}{\mu_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}\right)\,.

The proof is deferred to
Section

E.1
.

5.2
Taylor expansion with respect to the error terms

In this step, we use the Neumann series to expand
\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\right)^{\dagger}_{\uparrow}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\right)_{\downarrow}
and insert the formula in Eq. (
5.5
). Upon organizing various terms and controlling all higher-order terms (as detailed in
Lemma

5.3
), we conclude in
Lemma

5.4
that proving
Theorem

1.10
hinges on establishing:

\left\|(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\left(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)\right\|_{2}\sim n^{-1.5}\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\left(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)\right\|_{2}\sim n^{-1.5}\,.

(5.6)

Without loss of generality, we assume
\boldsymbol{U}_{r}=\boldsymbol{I}_{r}
. For convenience, we define some notions for the error terms appearing in the expansion:

\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}=\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r},\quad\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}=\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}(:-1,:r),\quad\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}=\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}(1:,:r)\,.

Recall

\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}=\boldsymbol{Q}(:n-1,:r),\quad\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{\uparrow}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}(:n-1,:r),\quad\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}=\boldsymbol{Q}(1:,:r),\quad\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{\downarrow}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}(1:,:r)

It’s easy to check that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}=\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{2}\right)_{\uparrow},\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}=\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{2}\right)_{\downarrow}\,.

(5.7)

The following lemma provides a second-order truncation for the Neumann series expansion (
Definition

A.6
) of
\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}^{+}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}
:

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{P}
be defined as Eq. (
5.2
). Then, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}^{+}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{Q}^{+}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\left((\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}+\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)-\left((\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}+\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}\right)(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}

(first order terms)

\displaystyle+(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow})-\left((\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}+\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}\right)\left((\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}+\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)

(second order terms)

\displaystyle+\left(-(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow})+\left((\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}+\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}\right)^{2}\right)(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}

(second order terms)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{3}\log^{3}n}{\mu^{3}_{r}(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n)^{1.5}}\right)\,,

(5.8)

By re-grouping and upper-bounding some high-order terms, we obtain the key result of this sub-section:

Lemma 5.4
.

It holds that

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}^{+}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{Q}^{+}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right\|_{2}

(5.9)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\|(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\left(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)\right\|_{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(1+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}}\right)\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\left(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{3}\log^{3}n}{\mu^{3}_{r}(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n)^{1.5}}\right)\,.

The proofs
Lemmas

5.3
and
5.4
of are deferred to
Section

E.2
.

5.3
Error cancellation in the Taylor expansion

In this step, we will prove
Eq.

5.6
. Because
\left\|\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}\sim n^{-0.5}
(
Eq.

5.4
), it suffices to establish:

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right\|_{2}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r^{1.5}\alpha}{\mu_{r}n}+\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right),

(5.10)

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\left(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)\right\|_{2}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r^{2}\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n)^{1.5}}\right)\,.

We first define the errors in the column space of
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
and orthogonal to this subspace:

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}:=\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{n}-\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\right)\boldsymbol{E}_{1}^{k}\boldsymbol{E}_{2}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-1}\right)^{k+1}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\,.

(5.11a)

and

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{2}:={\cal O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\perp}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\,.

(5.11b)

Then, we immediately have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}=\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{2}\right)_{\uparrow},\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}=\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{2}\right)_{\downarrow}\,.

(5.12)

Proof of the first equation in
Eq.

5.10
.

Since
\left\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{2}\right\|_{2}\sim n^{-1}
by its definition, we only need to consider
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}_{1}^{Q})_{\uparrow}-(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}_{1}^{Q})_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}
. A key observation is that
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
approximates
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})
closely (see
Lemma

1.8
), implying
(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\approx\mathrm{diag}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}^{-1})
. After expanding
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}_{1}^{Q})_{\uparrow}-(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}_{1}^{Q})_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}
, we obtain the following lemma:

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{P}_{v}
be defined in
Lemma

1.8
. We have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right\|_{2}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(\Big\|\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{P}^{\dagger}_{v}\right)_{\uparrow}-\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\boldsymbol{P}\right)_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{\dagger}_{v}\Big\|_{2}+\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\,,

(5.13a)

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{P}^{\dagger}_{v}=

\displaystyle\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\boldsymbol{E}_{1}^{k}\boldsymbol{E}_{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-(k+1)}

(5.13b)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{E}_{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}\right)\,,

and

\displaystyle\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\boldsymbol{P}\right)_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{\dagger}_{v}

(5.13c)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\boldsymbol{E}_{1}^{k}\boldsymbol{E}_{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-(k+1)}\right)_{\downarrow}\diag(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}^{-1})

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{E}_{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\right)_{\downarrow}\diag(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}^{-1})

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}\right)\,.

Then, we find that the main components take the form:

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{A}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{A}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})z^{-1}_{i}\,,

where
\boldsymbol{A}
is a Toeplitz matrix satisfying
\boldsymbol{A}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-0.5})
and
\sup_{i,j}\left|\boldsymbol{A}_{i,j}\right|=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z)\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
is defined as
(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z))_{i}=z^{i}
. By leveraging the structure of Toeplitz matrices, we obtain:

\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})-\boldsymbol{A}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})z^{-1}_{i}\right)_{k}\right\|_{\infty}=\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left(-\boldsymbol{A}_{k+1,1}z^{-1}_{i}+\overline{\boldsymbol{A}_{k,n}}z^{n-1}_{i}\right)\right\|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1.5})

for
1\leq k\leq n-1
. This implies:

\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{A}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{A}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})z^{-1}_{i}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})

instead of the trivial bound
\mathcal{O}(n^{-0.5})
.
Based on this intuition, we obtain the following lemma:

Lemma 5.6
.

\displaystyle\Big\|\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{P}^{\dagger}_{v}\right)_{\uparrow}-\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\boldsymbol{P}\right)_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{\dagger}_{v}\Big\|_{2}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r^{1.5}\alpha}{\mu_{r}n}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}\right)\,.

(5.14)

Proofs
Lemmas

5.5
and
5.6
of are deferred to
Section

E.3.1
. They immediately imply the first equation of Eq. (
5.10
).

Corollary 5.7
.

It holds that

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right\|_{2}=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r^{1.5}\alpha}{\mu_{r}n}+\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)

(5.15)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\Big\|\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{P}^{\dagger}_{v}\right)_{\uparrow}-\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\boldsymbol{P}\right)_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{\dagger}_{v}\Big\|_{2}+\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r^{1.5}\alpha}{\mu_{r}n}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}+\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r^{1.5}\alpha}{\mu_{r}n}+\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\,,

where the first step follows from Eq. (
5.13a
), the second step follows from Eq. (
5.14
).
∎

Proof of the second equation in
Eq.

5.10
.

The idea is similar to the proof of the first equation. First, we (approximately) decompose
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\left(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)
into four terms:

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\boldsymbol{V}_{r}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})
. We have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\left(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)\right\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(\left\|\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\uparrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\right)_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\uparrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{2}\boldsymbol{P}\right)_{\uparrow}\right\|_{2}\right.

(5.16)

\displaystyle+\left\|\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\downarrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\right)_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\downarrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{2}\boldsymbol{P}\right)_{\downarrow}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\left.\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n)^{1.5}}\right)\,.

Then, we upper-bound the four terms.

•

For
\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\uparrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{2}\boldsymbol{P}\right)_{\uparrow}
and
\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\downarrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{2}\boldsymbol{P}\right)_{\downarrow}
, first notice that
(\boldsymbol{V}_{r})^{\dagger}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}_{2}^{Q})=\boldsymbol{0}
, since
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{2}\boldsymbol{P}\propto\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}^{\perp}_{r}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}
and the column spaces of
\boldsymbol{V}_{r}
and
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
are the same. Although the matrices in these two terms involve
\uparrow
and
\downarrow
, we are still able to show that their norms are small.

•

For
\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\uparrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\boldsymbol{P}\right)_{\uparrow}
and
\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\downarrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\boldsymbol{P}\right)_{\downarrow}
, we use the structural properties of
\boldsymbol{V}_{r}
,
\boldsymbol{E}_{1}
, and
\boldsymbol{E}_{2}
to bound each entry of these terms, which will lead to the spectral norm bounds.

More specifically, we establish the following lemma:

Lemma 5.9
.

\left\|\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\uparrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{2}\boldsymbol{P}\right)_{\uparrow}\right\|_{2}\leq{\cal O}\left(\frac{r\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}\right),~~~\left\|\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\uparrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\right)_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\left(r+\frac{\sqrt{r}}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}n^{1.5}}\right)\,,

(5.17a)

and

\left\|\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\downarrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{2}\boldsymbol{P}\right)_{\downarrow}\right\|_{2}\leq{\cal O}\left(\frac{r\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}\right),~~~\left\|\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\downarrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\right)_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\left(r+\frac{\sqrt{r}}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}n^{1.5}}\right)\,,

(5.17b)

Proofs of
Lemmas

5.8
and
5.9
are deferred to
Section

E.3.2
. They imply the second inequality of Eq. (
5.10
).

Corollary 5.10
.

It holds that

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\left(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)\right\|_{2}=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n)^{1.5}}\right)\,.

(5.18)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\left(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)\right\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\left\|\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\uparrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\right)_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\uparrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{2}\boldsymbol{P}\right)_{\uparrow}\right\|_{2}\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\left\|\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\downarrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{1}\right)_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\left((\boldsymbol{V}_{r})_{\downarrow}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{Q}_{2}\boldsymbol{P}\right)_{\downarrow}\right\|_{2}\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n)^{1.5}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}+\frac{r\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}n^{1.5}}+\frac{\sqrt{r}\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n)^{1.5}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n)^{1.5}}\right)\,,

where the first step follows from Eq. (
5.16
), and the second step follows from Eq. (
5.17a
) and Eq. (
5.17b
).
∎

5.4
Proof of
Theorem

5.1

Proof.

First, the constructions of the matrices
\boldsymbol{U}_{r}
and
\boldsymbol{P}
are shown in
Lemma

5.2
. Since
\boldsymbol{U}_{r}
is an orthogonal matrix that changes the basis of
\boldsymbol{Q}
, without loss of generality, we may consider
\boldsymbol{U}_{r}=\boldsymbol{I}_{r}
. It remains to prove that Eq. (
5.1
) holds for this
\boldsymbol{P}
.

By
Lemma

5.4
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}^{+}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{Q}^{+}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\left\|(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\left(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)\right\|_{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(1+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}}\right)\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\left(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{3}\log^{3}n}{\mu^{3}_{r}(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n)^{1.5}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\left\|\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)\right\|_{2}+\mathcal{O}(1)\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\left(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{3}\log^{3}n}{\mu^{3}_{r}(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n)^{1.5}}\right)\,,

where we use
\left\|(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}\right\|_{2}
and
n=\Omega\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)
in the second step.

By Eq. (
5.4
) and Eq. (
5.15
),

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)\right\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}}\cdot\left(\frac{r^{1.5}\alpha}{\mu_{r}n}+\frac{\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~{\cal O}\left(\frac{r^{1.5}\alpha^{2}\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{0.5}n^{1.5}}+\frac{\alpha^{3}\log^{1.5}n}{\mu^{3}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{1.5}n^{1.5}}\right)\,.

And by Eq. (
5.18
),

\displaystyle{\cal O}(1)\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\left(\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\uparrow}-\boldsymbol{E}^{Q}_{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right)\right\|_{2}=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n)^{1.5}}\right)\,.

Thus, we obtain that

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}^{+}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{Q}^{+}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle~{\cal O}\left(\frac{\alpha^{3}\log^{1.5}n}{\mu^{3}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{1.5}n^{1.5}}+\frac{r^{1.5}\alpha^{2}\log n}{\mu^{2}_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{1.5}}\right)\,,

which completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Appendix A
Preliminaries

A.1
Notation

We use
\mathrm{i}\coloneqq\sqrt{-1}
to denote the imaginary unit. For a complex number
z=a+b\mathrm{i}\in\mathbb{C}
, we use
\overline{z}=a-b\mathrm{i}
to denote the complex conjugate of
z
.

We use
\mathbb{T}
to denote the unit circle in the complex plane. We use
\|\cdot\|_{2}
to denote the matrix spectral norm and
\|\cdot\|_{\rm F}
to denote the Frobenius norm.

\left\|\cdot\right\|_{p}
denotes the vector
p
-norm.

For a matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times m}
, we use
\boldsymbol{A}(:a,:b)
to denote the submatrix with rows from 1 to
a
and columns from 1 to
b
and define
\boldsymbol{A}_{r}
as the matrix that contains first
r
columns of
\boldsymbol{A}
. We use
\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger}
to represent the Hermitian transpose (or conjugate transpose) of
\boldsymbol{A}
, i.e.,
(\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger})_{ij}=\overline{\boldsymbol{A}_{ji}}
. In addition,
\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{A})
means the
i
-th eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{A}
,
\sigma_{i}(\boldsymbol{A})
means the
i
-th singular value of
\boldsymbol{A}
,
\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{A})
means the smallest nonzero eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{A}
if
\boldsymbol{A}
is nonnegative definite, and
\sigma_{\min}(\boldsymbol{A})
means the smallest nonzero singular value of
\boldsymbol{A}
. For a matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
with orthonormal columns, we use
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{A}}
and
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{A}^{\perp}}
to denote the projectors to the column space and the orthogonal subspace:

\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger},\quad\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{A}^{\perp}}\coloneqq\mathbf{I}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{A}}.

For two matrices
\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
and some quantity
f\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we use
\boldsymbol{A}\leq\boldsymbol{B}+f
to denote
\left\|\boldsymbol{A}-\boldsymbol{B}\right\|_{2}\leq f
.

A.2
Pseudoinverses

We will make frequent use of the (Moore–Penrose) pseudoinverse:

Definition A.1
(Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse).

Let
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
be a matrix with SVD
\boldsymbol{A}=\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{V}^{\dagger}
. The
Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse
(or just pseudoinverse) of
\boldsymbol{A}
is defined as

\boldsymbol{A}^{+}=\boldsymbol{V}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{+}\boldsymbol{U}^{\dagger}\,,

where
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{+}
is an
n\times m
matrix with
(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{+})_{ij}=(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{ii})^{-1}
if
i=j
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{ii}\neq 0
and
(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{+})_{ij}=0
otherwise.

In this paper, we only use the pseudoinverse of full-rank (rectangular) matrices. In particular, if
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
is a matrix of full column rank, its pseudoinverse is

\boldsymbol{A}^{+}=\left(\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{A}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger}\,.

(A.1)

We will make use of the reverse order for the pseudoinverse
[
Gre66
]
:

Fact A.2
(Reverse order law of pseudoinverse).

For a full column-rank matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
and a full row-rank matrix
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times p}
,
(\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{B})^{+}=\boldsymbol{B}^{+}\boldsymbol{A}^{+}
.

Perturbation theory for pseudoinverses is provided by the following theorem
[
Han87
, eq. (21)]
:

Theorem A.3
(Perturbation for pseudoinverses).

Let
\boldsymbol{A}
be a matrix of full column rank and suppose that
\left\|\boldsymbol{E}\right\|_{2}<\sigma_{\rm min}(\boldsymbol{A})
.
Then

\left\|(\boldsymbol{A}+\boldsymbol{E})^{+}-\boldsymbol{A}^{+}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{3\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{A}^{+}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{E}\right\|_{2}}{1-\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{A}^{+}}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{E}\right\|_{2}}.

A.3
Matrix norms

In this paper, we mainly focus on the spectral norm of a matrix:
\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{2}\coloneqq\sup_{\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{2}=1}\|\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{x}\|_{2}
for any
\boldsymbol{M}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times m}
. We will frequently use the following two basic facts about the spectral norm:

Fact A.4
(Sub-multiplicativity of spectral norm).

For any
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times m}
and
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times p}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{B}\|_{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{2}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{2}\,.

Fact A.5
(Spectral norm of submatrix).

Let
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times m}
and
\boldsymbol{B}
be any sub-matrix of
\boldsymbol{A}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{2}\,.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\boldsymbol{A}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{B}&*\\
*&*\end{bmatrix}
, since permuting the columns and rows does not change the spectral norm. Then, we have

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{2}=\sup_{\left\|\boldsymbol{x}\right\|_{2}=1}~\left\|\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{x}\right\|_{2}\leq\sup_{|\boldsymbol{x}|_{2}=1}~\left\|\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{B}&*\\
*&*\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{x}&\boldsymbol{0}\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{2}\leq\sup_{\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{2}=1}~\left\|\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{y}\right\|_{2}=\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{2}\,.

The result is proven.
∎

A.4
Neumann series

The Neumann series is one of the most powerful tools in matrix analysis.
It is the matrix generalization of a geometric series.

Definition A.6
(Neumann series).

Given a matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
with
\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{2}<1
, the
Neumann series
of
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
is the convergent series representation

(\mathbf{I}_{n}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{A}^{k}.

A.5
Eigenvalue and eigenvector perturbation theory

Our argument will require several standard results from matrix perturbation theory.
First, we will make use of additive and multiplicative perturbation theorems for eigenvalues of Hermitian matrices.
For additive perturbation, we will use Weyl’s theorem:

Theorem A.7
(Weyl’s theorem).

Let
\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{E}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
be Hermitian matrices.
Then,

\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{A})-\left\|\boldsymbol{E}\right\|_{2}\leq\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{A}+\boldsymbol{E})\leq\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{A})+\left\|\boldsymbol{E}\right\|_{2}\quad\text{for }i=1,2,\ldots,n.

For multiplicative perturbation theory, we will use Ostrowski’s theorem.

Theorem A.8
(Ostrowski’s theorem).

Let
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
be a nonsingular Hermitian matrix and
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times r}
have full column-rank.
Then,
\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{B}^{\dagger}
has
r
nonzero eigenvalues satisfying the bounds

\sigma_{\rm min}^{2}(\boldsymbol{B})\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{A})\leq\lambda_{i}\left(\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{B}^{\dagger}\right)\leq\sigma_{\rm max}^{2}(\boldsymbol{B})\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{A})\quad\text{for }i=1,2,\ldots,r.

Ostrowski’s theorem for the case in which
\boldsymbol{A}
and
\boldsymbol{B}
are both square appears in
[
HJ12
, Thm. 4.5.9]
.
The generalization to
\boldsymbol{B}
rectangular presented here is straightforward.

Next, we will need perturbation bounds of eigenspaces of Hermitian matrices.
The classical measure of the distance between subspaces is the principal angle, which admits multiple equivalent definitions
[
SS90
, Sec. I.5]
.
We use the following definition: for matrices
\boldsymbol{U}
and
\boldsymbol{V}
with orthonormal columns, the sin of the principal angle between the ranges of
\boldsymbol{U}
and
\boldsymbol{V}
is

\sin\theta(\boldsymbol{U},\boldsymbol{V})\coloneqq\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{U}^{\dagger}-\boldsymbol{V}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{V}^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}.

With this definition in hand, we now state the classical result for the perturbation of eigenspaces of Hermitian matrices, due to a Davis and Kahan

[
DK70
,
\sin\theta
theorem]
:

Theorem A.9
(Davis–Kahan
\sin\theta
theorem).

Let
\boldsymbol{T}
,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{T}}
,
\boldsymbol{Q}
,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}
be defined as
Section

A.1
,

\sin\theta\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r},\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}\right)\leq\frac{\left\|\boldsymbol{E}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}}{\lambda_{r}(\boldsymbol{T})-\lambda_{r+1}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}})}.

The following result (see, e.g., the proof of
[
Moi15
, Lem. 2.7]
) shows that a bound on the angle between subspaces yields a bound between
\boldsymbol{Q}
and
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}
, after multiplication by a unitary matrix.

Proposition A.10
(Distance from angles).

Let
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
and
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}
be matrices with orthonormal columns.
There exists a unitary matrix such that

\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}\right\|_{2}\leq 2\sin\theta\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r},\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}\right)=2\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}-\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}.

Last, we will need one result for the perturbation of diagonalizable matrices, a version of the famous Bauer–Fike theorem augmented by Gerschgorin and Ostrowski’s continuity argument.
We take this result from
[
SS90
]
:

Theorem A.11
(Bauer–Fike theorem,
[
SS90
, Thm. IV.3.3]
).

Let
\boldsymbol{A}=\boldsymbol{V}\diag(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\boldsymbol{V}^{-1}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
be a diagonalizable matrix and let
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\lambda}}
be the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{A}+\boldsymbol{E}
.
Then

\max_{1\leq i\leq r}\min_{1\leq j\leq r}|\widehat{\lambda}_{i}-\lambda_{j}|\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{V}\right\|_{2}\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{V}^{-1}}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{E}\right\|_{2}.

Moreover, we have the following bound in the optimal matching distance, defined in
Eq.

1.9
:

\operatorname{md}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\widehat{\lambda}})\leq(2r-1)\left\|\boldsymbol{V}\right\|_{2}\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{V}^{-1}}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{E}\right\|_{2}.

A.6
Spectral perturbation via resolvents

The last results that we will need from classical matrix perturbation theory are formulas for spectral projectors using contour integrals and resolvents.
For this section, let
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
be a Hermitian matrix with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}
associated to orthonormal eigenvectors
\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{q}_{n}
.

The core object is the resolvent:

Definition A.12
(Resolvent).

The resolvent function
\boldsymbol{R}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\}\to\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
of
\boldsymbol{A}
is

\boldsymbol{R}(\zeta)\coloneqq(\zeta\mathbf{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}.

For simplcity, we shall often omit “
\mathbf{I}
” and write the resolvent merely as
\boldsymbol{R}(\zeta)=(\zeta-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}
.

We first state a lemma that estimates the locations of the perturbed eigenvalues via the resolvent method.

Lemma A.13
(Eigenvalue perturbation via resolvents,
[
Gil22
, Lemma 2.1]
, see also
[
Kat12
, Theorem IV.3.18]
).

Let
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{A}}
be its perturbation. For any
i\in[r]
, suppose the
i
-th eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{A}
is
2\delta
-separated from other eigenvalues, i.e.,
\min_{j\neq i}|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}|\geq 2\delta>0
. Let
{\cal C}
be a circle in the complex plane centered at
\lambda_{i}
with radius
\delta
. If the following condition holds:

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{A}}-\boldsymbol{A}\right\|_{2}\cdot\sup_{z\in{\cal C}}\left\|(z\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\right\|_{2}<1\,,

then
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{A}}
also has exactly one eigenvalue contained in
{\cal C}
.

Next, we present the following results that express the spectral projector as the contour
integral of the resolvent:

Theorem A.14
(Spectral theory via resolvents,
[
Kat12
, Sec. I.5]
).

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a simple closed curve in
\mathbb{C}
such that
\lambda_{i_{1}},\ldots,\lambda_{i_{\ell}}
lie within the interior of
\mathcal{C}
and all other eigenvalues lie outside
\mathcal{C}
.
Then

\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\boldsymbol{q}_{i_{j}}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{q}_{i_{j}}^{\dagger}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta.

Here, as always,
\oint_{\mathcal{C}}
denotes a contour integral around the curve
\mathcal{C}
, traversed counterclockwise.

This result follows immediately from the Cauchy integral formula and the following spectral representation of the resolvent
(\zeta-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\zeta-\lambda_{i})^{-1}\boldsymbol{q}_{i}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{q}_{i}^{\dagger}
.

Theorem

A.14
can be used to derive a convergent perturbation series for spectral projectors of a perturbation
\boldsymbol{A}+\boldsymbol{E}
of
\boldsymbol{A}
by expanding the resolvent
(\zeta-(\boldsymbol{A}+\boldsymbol{E}))^{-1}
in a Neumann series in
\boldsymbol{E}
.
Here is one such result:

Lemma A.15
(Perturbation theory via resolvents).

Assume
\boldsymbol{A}
is rank-
r
,
\lambda_{r+1}=\cdots=\lambda_{n}=0
and let
\boldsymbol{A}+\boldsymbol{E}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
be a Hermitian perturbation of
\boldsymbol{A}
with eigenvalues
\widehat{\lambda}_{1}\geq\cdots\widehat{\lambda}_{n}
with associated eigenvectors
\boldsymbol{\widehat{q}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\widehat{q}}_{n}
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a simple closed curve in
\mathbb{C}
such that
\mathcal{C}

\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r},\widehat{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\lambda}_{r}
lie inside
\mathcal{C}
and all other eigenvalues of both
\boldsymbol{A}
and
\boldsymbol{A}+\boldsymbol{E}
lie outside
\mathcal{C}
.
Assume that

\left\|\boldsymbol{E}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\right\|_{2}<1\quad\text{for }\zeta\in\mathcal{C}.

Then, denoting
\boldsymbol{\Pi}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\boldsymbol{q}_{i}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{q}_{i}^{\dagger}
and
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Pi}}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\boldsymbol{\widehat{q}}_{i}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{\widehat{q}}_{i}^{\dagger}
, we have

\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Pi}}=\boldsymbol{\Pi}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{E}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\right)^{k}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{C}
is a simple closed curve, it is compact.
Therefore, there exists
0<\alpha<1
such that

\left\|\boldsymbol{E}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq\alpha\quad\text{for }\zeta\in\mathcal{C}.

Therefore, the resolvent
(\zeta-(\boldsymbol{A}+\boldsymbol{E}))^{-1}
has a Neumann expansion

(\zeta-(\boldsymbol{A}+\boldsymbol{E}))^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\zeta-\left((\zeta-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\boldsymbol{E}\right)^{k}\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}.

that is uniformly convergent on
\mathcal{C}
.
Therefore, integrating both sides we obtain

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}(\zeta-(\boldsymbol{A}+\boldsymbol{E}))^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{E}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\right)^{k}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta.

By
Theorem

A.14
, the left-hand side is
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Pi}}
and the first term in the right-hand side is
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
:

\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Pi}}=\boldsymbol{\Pi}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{E}(\zeta-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\right)^{k}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta.

This is the stated conclusion.
∎

Appendix B
Vandermonde matrices

In this section, we discuss results for Vandermonde matrices that will be heavily used throughout our analysis.
We begin in
Section

B.1
by discussing Moitra’s bounds
[
Moi15
]
for the singular values of a Vandermonde matrix.
Then, in
Section

B.2
, we present a comparison lemma for the Vandermonde basis and eigenbasis of a Toeplitz matrix.

B.1
Moitra’s singular value bounds

For us, one of the core technical tools for analyzing ESPRIT is bound on the singular values of Vandermonde matrices, proven in the seminal work of Moitra
[
Moi15
]
.
The main result is as follows:

Theorem B.1
(Vandermonde matrix: singular values,
adapted from
[
Moi15
, Thm. 1.1]
).

Let
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{T}^{s}
be a set of frequencies and define a gap

\delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}\coloneqq\min_{i\neq j}|z_{i}-z_{j}|.

Then if
n>1+2\pi/\delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}
, the largest and smallest singular values of
\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z})
satisfy

\sigma_{\rm max}(\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}))\leq\sqrt{n-1+\frac{2\pi}{\delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}},\quad\sigma_{\rm min}(\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}))\geq\sqrt{n-1-\frac{2\pi}{\delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}}.

Moitra’s original results were stated in terms of the minimum separation of the
z_{i}
’s in the wrap-around metric; here, we have transferred these results to use the standard distance on
\mathbb{C}
.

This result immediately yields the following useful corollary:

Corollary B.2
(Vandermonde matrix: Gram matrix and outer product).

In the setting of
Theorem

B.1
,

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{n}\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z})^{\dagger}\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z})-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right),\quad\left\|\frac{1}{n}\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z})\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z})^{\dagger}-\mathbf{I}_{n}\right\|_{2}\leq 1\,.

B.2
Vandermonde basis and eigenbasis

The exact Toeplitz matrix
\boldsymbol{T}

Eq.

1.6
has two useful factorizations, each of which packages useful information about the matrix.
The first factorization is the eigendecomposition

\boldsymbol{T}=\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{Q}^{\dagger}\quad\text{for }\boldsymbol{Q}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n},\>\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\in\real^{n\times n}.

(B.1)

The eigendecomposition is useful because it can be easily computed using standard methods from numerical linear algebra.
The second useful factorization is the
Vandermonde decomposition

\boldsymbol{T}=\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}.

The Vandermonde decomposition contains information about the frequency spectrum.
However, it can only be computed indirectly, such as by procedures such as ESPRIT which use the eigendecomposition as a subroutine.

Recall that

\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}:=\min_{1\leq i\leq r,1\leq j\leq d,i\neq j}|z_{i}-z_{j}|\leq\min_{1\leq i,j\leq r,i\neq j}|z_{i}-z_{j}|.

A direct consequence of
Theorem

B.1
is the following estimates for the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{T}
:

Corollary B.3
(Toeplitz matrix: eigenvalues).

Assume
n>1+2\pi/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}
.
It holds that

\lambda_{1}(\boldsymbol{T})=\mu_{1}\left(n-1+\frac{2\pi}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right),\quad\lambda_{r}(\boldsymbol{T})=\mu_{r}\left(n-1-\frac{2\pi}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\,,

(B.2)

and
\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{T})=0
for
i=r+1,\ldots,n
.

Proof.

Applying Ostrowski’s theorem (
Theorem

A.8
) to the Vandermonde decomposition
\boldsymbol{T}=\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\cdot\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}
gives that for any
1\leq i\leq r
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\min}^{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}))\cdot\mu_{i}\leq\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{T})\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}))\cdot\mu_{i}\,.

By
Theorem

B.1
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\boldsymbol{T})\leq\mu_{1}\left(n-1+\frac{2\pi}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right),\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda_{r}(\boldsymbol{T})\geq\mu_{r}\left(n-1-\frac{2\pi}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\,.

The corollary then follows.
∎

The essential fact that we will make frequent use of throughout our analysis is that when
n\gg 1/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}
, the eigenbasis
\boldsymbol{Q}
and the scaled Vandermonde basis
\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})/\sqrt{n}
are equivalent up to multiplication by a nearly unitary matrix
\boldsymbol{P}_{v}
.
The result is as follows:
See
1.8

Proof.

Specifically, because the image of
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
equals to the image of
\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})
by the definition of
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
, there exists an invertible matrix
\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\,.

By multiplying both sides of this equation by the pseudoinverse of
\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})/\sqrt{n}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}_{v}:=\left(\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{V}^{\dagger}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}^{\dagger}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\,.

(B.3)

It remains to prove
Eq.

1.14
.
Notice that the matrices
\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}-\mathbf{I}_{r}
and
\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}-\mathbf{I}_{r}
are adjoints of each other. Thus, we only need to prove one of the equality in
Eq.

1.14
.
Consider the second equality. We have

\begin{split}\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}=&~\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}\\
=&~\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}\left(\mathbf{I}_{r}-\frac{\boldsymbol{V}^{\dagger}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})}{n}\right)\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\right\|_{2}\\
=&~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\|_{2}\right)\\
=&~{\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\cdot\left(\|\boldsymbol{P}^{\dagger}_{v}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}-\mathbf{I}_{r}\|_{2}+\|\mathbf{I}_{r}\|_{2}\right)\right)\\
=&~{\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{\dagger}_{v}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}+{\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\,.\end{split}

(B.4)

The first step follows from the fact that
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
has orthonormal columns,
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}=\mathbf{I}_{r}
.
The second step follows from the identity
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}=\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{P}_{v}/\sqrt{n}
, the submultipicative property of the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2}
, and the fact that
\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}^{2}
.
The third step follows from
Corollary

B.2
.
The fourth step follows from triangle inequality.
Now, rearrange
Eq.

B.4
to obtain the desired conclusion

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}={\cal O}\left(\frac{1/(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n)}{1-1/(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n)}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\,.

The lemma is then proved.
∎

As a corollary of this result, we obtain a result for
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}
, defined to be
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}
with its first row deleted.

Corollary B.4
(Approximate unitarity of
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}
).

Assuming
n>1+2\pi/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}
, it holds that

\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right).

(B.5)

In addition,

\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}\right\|_{2},\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1).

(B.6)

Proof.

Utilizing the matrix from
Lemma

1.8
, we have

\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}.

By the definition of the Vandermonde matrix,

\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})_{\downarrow}=\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\cdot\boldsymbol{D}\quad\text{for }\boldsymbol{D}=\diag(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}).

Thus, we obtain

\begin{split}\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}&=\left\|\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{D}^{\dagger}\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{D}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}\\
&\leq\left\|\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{-1}\boldsymbol{D}^{\dagger}\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{D}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{n}\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}-\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{-1}\right\|_{2}\left\|\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\right\|_{2}.\end{split}

(B.7)

For the inequality, we use the triangle inequality and the submultiplicative property of the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2}
.
To bound
\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}-\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{-1}\right\|_{2}
, employ the submultiplicative property again and use
Lemma

1.8
:

\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}-\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{-1}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right).

Using this bound,
Theorem

B.1
to bound
\left\|\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right\|_{2}
, and
Lemma

1.8
to bound
\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\right\|_{2}
,
Eq.

B.7
simplifies to

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{-1}\boldsymbol{D}^{\dagger}\left(\frac{1}{n}\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right)\boldsymbol{D}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\right\|_{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{n}\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}+\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\,.

By
Theorem

B.1
, the eigenvalues of
\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})
within a range
n\pm\mathcal{O}(1/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}})
.
Thus, the eigenvalues of
\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})/n-\mathbf{I}_{r}
are within a range
\pm\mathcal{O}(\tfrac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n})
, from which it follows that

\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{n}\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})-\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}+\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right).

This is the first conclusion
Eq.

B.5
.

The conclusion
Eq.

B.5
entails that the singular values of
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}
satisfy the following bound

\left|\sigma_{j}^{2}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})-1\right|=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,r.

Therefore, it follows that
\sigma_{j}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow})=\Theta(1)
for each
j
, from which
Eq.

B.6
follows.
∎

Appendix C
Deferred proofs for
Section

2

In this section, we prove
Lemmas

2.1
and
2.2
from
Section

2
.
We begin in
Section

C.1
by proving some supplementary error bounds that we will use.
Then, we prove
Lemma

2.1
in
Section

C.2
and
Lemma

2.2
in
Section

C.3
.

C.1
Error matrix bounds

The error matrix
\boldsymbol{E}=\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}+\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}
neatly decomposes into a sum of two terms.
In this section, we develop appropriate bounds for each of these terms separately.

For
\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}
, we employ the following estimate for the norm of the Toeplitz matrix of random errors:

Lemma C.1
(Norm of a random Toeplitz matrix).

Assume that the errors
\{E_{j}\}_{j=0}^{n-1}
satisfy condition Eq. (
1.4
).
Then, for a universal constant
C>0
,

\prob\left\{\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\right\|_{2}\geq C\alpha\sqrt{n\log n}\right\}\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}.

If the random variables
E_{j}
are Gaussian, we have the lower bound
\expect[\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\right\|_{2}]=\Omega(\alpha\sqrt{n\log n})
, so this result is sharp.

This result appears in the proof of
[
Mec07
, Thm. 2 (Page 320)]
for
E_{j}
real.
Estimates of this form with explicit constants in the real or complex case can easily be obtained from the theory of matrix concentration (see, e.g.,
[
Tro15
, sec. 4.4]
for a specific calculation).

Using this result, we can bound the norm of the error matrix in total:

Proposition C.2
(Error matrix, norm bound).

It holds that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\|_{2}\leq n\mu_{\rm tail}.

(C.1)

Assume the hypotheses of
Lemma

C.1
.
Then, with probability at least
1-1/n
,

\left\|\boldsymbol{E}\right\|_{2}=n\mu_{\rm tail}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha\sqrt{n\log n}).

Proof.

Invoke the triangle inequality and the fact that
\left\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z)\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z)^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}=n
for any
z\in\mathbb{T}
:

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\|_{2}\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{d}\left\|\mu_{i}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}=\sum_{i=r+1}^{d}\mu_{i}\cdot n=n\mu_{\rm tail},

where the last step follows from the definition of
\mu_{\rm tail}
in
Eq.

1.3
.

Combining this with
Lemma

C.1
, we get that

\left\|\boldsymbol{E}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\right\|+\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm random}\right\|_{2}\leq n\mu_{\rm tail}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha\sqrt{n\log n})\,.

This is the desired conclusion.
∎

Finally, we need the following specialized bound on
\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}
:

Proposition C.3
(Tail error in the dominant eigenspace).

We have the bound

\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right).

Proof.

Write the Vandermonde decomposition of
\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}
in outer product form and use the triangle inequality, obtaining

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}=

\displaystyle~\left\|\sum_{i=r+1}^{n}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})\mu_{i}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{i=r+1}^{d}\mu_{i}\left\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sqrt{n}\mu_{\rm tail}\cdot\max_{r+1\leq i\leq d}~\left\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}\,,

where the second step follows from
\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z)\|_{2}=\sqrt{n}
for any
z\in\mathbb{T}
.

By
Lemma

1.8
,
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}=\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{P}_{v}/\sqrt{n}
, where
\boldsymbol{P}_{v}
satisfies
\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
.
Thus,

\max_{r+1\leq i\leq d}~\left\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\max_{r+1\leq i\leq d}~\left\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})^{\dagger}\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right\|_{2}\right).

Consider any
i\in\{r+1,\dots,d\}
. Notice that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})^{\dagger}\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})=\left(\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{w})^{\dagger}\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{w})-n\mathbf{I}_{r+1}\right)(r+1,:r)\,,

where
\boldsymbol{w}=(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}},z_{i})\in\mathbb{C}^{r+1}
. Moreover, by the definition of
\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}
, we know that
\min_{1\leq j\leq r}|z_{i}-z_{j}|\geq\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}
. Thus, invoking
Corollary

B.2
and
Fact

A.5
gives

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(z_{i})^{\dagger}\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right\|_{2}\leq n\cdot\left\|\frac{1}{n}\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{w})^{\dagger}\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{w})-\mathbf{I}_{r+1}\right\|_{2}={\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\,.

Therefore,

\left\|\boldsymbol{E}_{\rm tail}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{n}\mu_{\rm tail}\cdot{\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\,.

The desired result has been established.
∎

C.2
Proof of
Lemma

2.1

In this section, we provide a proof of
Lemma

2.1
, broken into two steps.

Step 1: Proof of Eq. (
2.1
).

The result of Eq. (
2.1
) follows directly by combining matrix perturbation theorems (
Section

A.5
), singular value bounds for Vandermonde matrices (
Section

B.1
), and the error matrix bounds developed in
Section

C.1
.

By Weyl’s theorem (
Theorem

A.7
) and
Proposition

C.2
,

\lambda_{r+1}\left(\boldsymbol{\widehat{T}}\right)\leq\lambda_{r+1}(\boldsymbol{T})+\left\|\boldsymbol{E}\right\|_{2}\leq n\mu_{\rm tail}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha\sqrt{n\log n})~~.

By Ostrowski’s theorem (
Theorem

A.8
) and
Theorem

B.1
,

\lambda_{r}(\boldsymbol{T})\geq\lambda_{r}(\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}}))\sigma_{\rm min}(\Vand(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}))^{2}\geq\mu_{r}\left(n-1-\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)=\Omega(\mu_{r}n).

In the final equality, we use the condition
n=\Omega(1/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}})
.
Invoking the previous two displays and, the generalized
\sin\theta
theorem (
Theorem

A.9
),
Proposition

C.3
, and the hypothesis
\mu_{r}=\Omega(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{tail}})
, we obtain

\sin\theta(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r},\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r})=\frac{\mathcal{O}(\mu_{\rm tail}/\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}+\alpha\sqrt{n\log n})}{\Omega(\mu_{r}n)}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\mu_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{n}}\right).

By
Proposition

A.10
, there exists a unitary matrix
\boldsymbol{U}_{r}
such that

\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}_{r}}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\right\|_{2}\leq 2\sin\theta(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r},\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r})=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\mu_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{n}}\right).

(C.2)

Step 2: Proof of
Eq.

2.2
.

Begin by invoking the triangle inequality and the submultiplicativity of
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2}
:

\begin{split}\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{U}_{r}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\right\|_{2}&\leq\left\|(\boldsymbol{\smash{\widehat{Q}}}_{\uparrow}^{+}-\boldsymbol{U}_{r}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+})\boldsymbol{\smash{\widehat{Q}}}_{\downarrow}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{U}_{r}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{U}_{r})\right\|_{2}\\
&\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{\smash{\widehat{Q}}}_{\uparrow}^{+}-\boldsymbol{U}_{r}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\right\|_{2}\end{split}

(C.3)

In the second inequality, we used the fact that adding a row to a matrix can only increase its norm, together with the fact that
\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}}_{r}\right\|_{2}=1
.

By
Theorem

A.3
, we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{\smash{\widehat{Q}}}_{\uparrow}^{+}-\boldsymbol{U}_{r}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{3\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}}\right\|_{2}}{1-\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}}\right\|_{2}\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{r}-\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{U}_{r}\|_{2}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\mu_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}+\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\log n}}{\mu_{r}\sqrt{n}}\right).

In the last bound, we use
Eqs.

C.2
and
B.6
.
Substituting this display into
Eq.

C.3
and using the bounds
Eqs.

B.6
and
C.2
again proves
Eq.

2.2
.

C.3
Proof of
Lemma

2.2

Utilizing the matrix
\boldsymbol{P}_{v}
defined in
Lemma

1.8
and the relations

\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})_{\uparrow}=\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}),\quad\Vand_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})_{\downarrow}=\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\diag(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\,,

we obtain the following diagonalization of
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}
:

\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}=\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{-1}\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{+}\Vand_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\diag(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{P}_{v}=\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{-1}\diag(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\,.

Observe that the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}
are
\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}
, which satisfy
\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}=\exp(\mathrm{i}\arg(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}))
. We also note that
\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}
are also the eigenvalues of

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}^{+}_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}=(\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{P})^{-1}\diag(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})(\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{P})\,.

Now, we apply the Bauer–Fike theorem (
Theorem

A.11
).
Let
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\lambda}}
be the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}
.
Then, by the optimal matching distance condition of the Bauer–Fike theorem (
Theorem

A.11
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{md}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{z}}_{r},\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\operatorname{md}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\lambda}},\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)

(C.4)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{-1}}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(r\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}\right)\,.

In the second line, the estimates
\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\right\|_{2},\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{-1}}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
from
Lemma

1.8
, the assumption
\left\|\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2},\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
, and the submultiplicative property of
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2}
.

Next, we prove the “Furthermore” part of
Lemma

2.2
. According to
Lemma

1.8
, we set
c\in(0,1)
such that
\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|(\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{P})^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{c}
. Suppose

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}<c\cdot\frac{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}{2}\,.

For any
i\in[r]
, define
{\cal C}:=\{\xi~|~|\xi-z_{i}|=\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}/2\}
. Then, for any
\xi\in{\cal C}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\xi\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}=

\displaystyle~\left\|(\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{P})^{-1}\cdot(\diag(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})-\xi\mathbf{I})^{-1}\cdot(\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{P})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|(\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{P})^{-1}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|(\diag(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})-\xi\mathbf{I})^{-1}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{c}\cdot\max_{j\in[r]}|z_{j}-\xi|^{-1}\leq\frac{2}{c\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\,,

where the last step follows from
\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}
is
\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}
-separated and
\xi\in{\cal C}
. Thus, we know that the condition of
Lemma

A.13
is satisfied:

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}\cdot\sup_{\xi\in{\cal C}}\left\|\left(\xi\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}<1.

By
Lemma

A.13
, there exists exactly one
j\in[r]
such that
|\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-z_{i}|<\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}/2
. Since the above argument works for any
i\in[r]
, we obtain that

\max_{i\in[r]}\min_{j\in[r]}~\left|\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-z_{i}\right|<\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}/2\,.

Because
\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}}
are
\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}
-separated, there exists a permutation
\pi
such that

\max_{i\in[r]}~\left|\widehat{\lambda}_{i}-z_{\pi(i)}\right|<\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}/2\,.

Also, for any
i\in[r]
and
j\neq i
,
\left|\widehat{\lambda}_{i}-z_{\pi(j)}\right|>\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}/2
.

Thus, by
Theorem

A.11
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq r}|\widehat{\lambda}_{i}-z_{\pi(i)}|=\max_{1\leq i\leq r}\min_{1\leq j\leq r}|\widehat{\lambda}_{i}-z_{j}|\leq

\displaystyle~{\cal O}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}\left\|\smash{\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}}\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~{\cal O}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}\right)\,,

which implies that

\displaystyle{\rm md}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{r},\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})={\cal O}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{\widehat{Q}}_{\downarrow}-\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\uparrow}^{+}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\downarrow}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|_{2}\right)\,.

The proof of the lemma is then completed.

Appendix D
Deferred proofs for
Section

4

This section provides supplementary proofs for
Section

4
.
In
Section

D.1
, we develop explicit formulas for the higher-order terms in the perturbation expansion result,
Proposition

4.2
.
In
Section

D.2
, we show how to bound the terms appearing in this expression.
We conclude by proving
Lemma

4.3
in
Section

D.3
.

D.1
Expansion of spectral projector: explicit formulas

In this section, we explicitly expand the perturbed projector
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}}
in terms of
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}
,
\boldsymbol{T}^{+}
,
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}^{\perp}_{r}}
, and
\boldsymbol{E}
, where
\boldsymbol{T}^{+}
is the pseudoinverse of
\boldsymbol{T}
. In particular, our goal is to identify the polynomial
\textbf{Poly}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}}
so that

\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}}=\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}}+\textbf{Poly}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}}\left(\boldsymbol{T}^{+},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\boldsymbol{Q}^{\perp}_{r}},\boldsymbol{E}\right)

(D.1)

A straightforward approach to computing
\textbf{Poly}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{r}}
involves exhaustively expanding each eigenvector projection within the contour integral (
Proposition

4.2
), then attempting to combine them to form
\boldsymbol{T}^{+}
. Interestingly, exploiting the inherent symmetry of the contour integral unexpectedly simplifies many complex expressions into a Schur polynomial, facilitating the combination of various projections.

To start, we first introduce the Schur polynomials:

Definition D.1
(Vandermonde determinant and Schur polynomials).

For numbers
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in\mathbb{C}
, the
Vandermonde determinant
is

W(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell})\coloneqq\det\begin{bmatrix}a_{1}^{\ell-1}&a_{2}^{\ell-1}&\cdots&a_{\ell-1}^{\ell-1}&a_{\ell}^{\ell-1}\\
a_{1}^{\ell-2}&a_{2}^{\ell-2}&\cdots&a_{\ell-1}^{\ell-2}&a_{\ell}^{\ell-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
a_{1}^{1}&a_{2}^{1}&\cdots&a_{\ell-1}^{1}&a_{\ell}^{1}\\
1&1&\cdots&1&1\end{bmatrix}=\prod_{i<j}(a_{i}-a_{j}).

For non-increasingly ordered nonnegative integers
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{\ell})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, the
Schur polynomial
is

s_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell})=\frac{1}{W(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell})}\det\begin{bmatrix}a_{1}^{\gamma_{1}+\ell-1}&a_{2}^{\gamma_{1}+\ell-1}&\cdots&a_{\ell-1}^{\gamma_{1}+\ell-1}&a_{\ell}^{\gamma_{1}+\ell-1}\\
a_{1}^{\gamma_{2}+\ell-2}&a_{2}^{\gamma_{2}+\ell-2}&\cdots&a_{\gamma_{2}+\ell-2}^{\ell-1}&a_{\ell}^{\gamma_{2}+\ell-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
