\displaystyle\left\|\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-(k-1)}\left(\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-1}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}-\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\right)\right\|_{2}

(E.59)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\cal{\displaystyle O}\left(\frac{1}{\mu_{r}n}\right)^{k-1}\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-1}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}-\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}\,.

Hence, to prove Eq. (
E.55b
), it suffices to bound

\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-1}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}-\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}.

Using the formula of
\boldsymbol{P}_{v}
in Eq. (
B.3
), we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-1}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}-\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}

(E.60)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{+}\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\right)\right)\right.

\displaystyle\cdot\left.{\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{+}}^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}\right)\,,

where
\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{+}=\left(\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}
is the pseudo-inverse of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})
, and the second step follows from
\Big\|\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{+}\Big\|_{2}={\cal O}(1)
by
Corollary

B.2
.

Since
\boldsymbol{T}=\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{Q}^{\dagger}=\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}\boldsymbol{Q}^{\dagger}_{r}
is the eigendecomposition, we notice that

\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}=\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}\,.

On the other hand, since
\boldsymbol{T}=\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\rm dom})\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{T}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left\|\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\rm dom})\left(\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})-\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\right)\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\rm dom})^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{1}}{\mu_{r}}\cdot\left\|\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})-\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\cal{\displaystyle O}\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\mu_{r}}\cdot\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\mu_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\,,

where the second step follows from
\Big\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\Big\|_{2}={\cal O}(1)
, the third step follows from
Corollary

B.2
, and the last step follows from
\mu_{1}={\Theta}(1)
. We further have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\left\|\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)\left(\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}-\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\,,

where the first step follows from
Lemma

1.8
, and the second step follows from Eq. (
1.14
). Combining the above three equations together, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{T}\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\right)\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}-\boldsymbol{T}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\mu_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\mu_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\mu_{r}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)\,.

Because

\mathrm{Ker}(\boldsymbol{T})=\mathrm{Ker}\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}\right)=\mathrm{Ker}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\right)

and

\mathrm{Range}(\boldsymbol{T})=\mathrm{Range}\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}\right)=\mathrm{Range}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\right)\,,

we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sigma_{\min}(\boldsymbol{T})^{-1}\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{T}\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}^{\dagger}-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\right)\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{2}}\right)\,,

where the second step follows from using
Corollary

B.3
. This proves the
k=1
case of Eq. (
E.55b
).

Finally, by induction
Eqs.

E.56
,
E.57
,
E.58
and
E.59
, we can prove that for any
k\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{-{k}}\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{\dagger}-\left(n\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{dom}})\right)^{-k}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~{\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{1}{\mu_{r}n}\right)^{k}\cdot\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n}\right)+\frac{1}{\mu_{r}n}\cdot\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\mu_{r}n}\right)^{k}\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}+{\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{\mu_{r}n}\right)^{k-1}\cdot\frac{1}{\mu_{r}^{2}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\mu_{r}n}\right)^{k+1}\frac{1}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\,.

This concludes the proof of Eq. (
E.55b
).
∎

Claim E.6
.

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{E}_{1}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\,.

Proof.

Recall that

\boldsymbol{E}_{1}=\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{tail}})\cdot\diag(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathrm{tail}})\cdot\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{tail}})^{\dagger}\,.

By
Proposition

C.3
, we have

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{E}_{1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\|_{2}={\cal O}\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\,.

Since
\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})\boldsymbol{P}_{v}
and
\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{-1}\|_{2}={\cal O}(1)
by
Lemma

1.8
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{V}(\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathrm{dom}})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{E}_{1}\right\|_{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{E}_{1}\boldsymbol{Q}_{r}\|_{2}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{P}_{v}^{-1}\|_{2}={\cal O}\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm tail}}{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)\,.

The claim then follows.
∎

Appendix F
Lower bound for spectral estimation

In this section, we prove a sample complexity lower bound for estimating the locations of the point sources in the spectral estimation problem. Information-theoretic lower bounds for spectral estimation have been extensively studied in prior works
[
SN89
,
SN90
,
Don92
,
Lee92
,
Moi15
,
DN15
,
PS15
,
KP16
,
LLF20
,
BDGY20
,
BGY21
,
LL21
,
LZGL22
]
. In particular,
[
SN89
]
proved a Cramér-Rao lower bound for estimation the location of a single point-source with i.i.d. Gaussian noise, showing that for any
unbiased estimator
using
n
samples, the variance is at least
1/n^{3}
. And
[
LL21
]
proved a min-max lower bound in terms of the “super-resolution factor”
1/(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{z}}n)
with
arbitrary
noise.
The settings of these lower bounds are somewhat different from ours and the proofs are complicated.
Below, we give a simple construction showing, that in our noisy super-resolution setting, the location estimation error is
\Omega(n^{-1.5})
and the intensity estimation error is
\Omega(n^{-0.5})
.

Theorem F.1
(Spectral estimation lower bound,
formal version of
Theorem

1.7
).

Let
\{g_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
be defined in Eq. (
1.1
), and
\{E_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\sim{\cal N}(0,1)
be i.i.d. standard Gaussians. Then,

•

Location estimation.
For any
\eta>0
, one cannot estimate the locations
\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
up to
\mathcal{O}(n^{-1.5-\eta})
matching distance error for all
i=1,\ldots,r
with constant success probability.

•

Intensity estimation.
For any
\eta>0
, one cannot estimate the intensities
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
within
\mathcal{O}(n^{-0.5-\eta})
matching distance error for all
i=1,\ldots,r
with constant success probability.

To prove this theorem, we will employ the following lemma
[
AAL23
, Thm. 1.8]
:

Lemma F.2
(TV distance of standard Gaussian distributions).

Let
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Then,

\displaystyle d_{\rm TV}\left({\cal N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}_{n}),{\cal N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2},\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}_{n})\right)\leq\|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}\|_{2}\,.

Proof of
Theorem

F.1
.

We first show lower bound for location estimation.
Let
\epsilon,\eta\in(0,1)
. Suppose there exists an algorithm that can estimate all the locations within
\epsilon={\cal O}(n^{-1.5-\eta})
error using
n
noisy measurements with constant success probability.
Consider two spectral densities with parameters
(\mu_{1}=1,z_{1}=e^{2i\epsilon})
and
(\mu_{1}^{\prime}=1,z_{1}^{\prime}=1)
, respectively. Since

\displaystyle|z_{1}-z^{\prime}_{1}|=|e^{2i\epsilon}-1|=2\sin(\epsilon)>\epsilon\,,

the algorithm can distinguish these two spectral densities with constant probability. On the other hand, it is easy to see that the
n
measurement outcomes for each spectral density are sampled from an
n
-dimensional Gaussian distribution
{\cal N}(\boldsymbol{g},\mathbf{I}_{n})
(or
{\cal N}(\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime},\mathbf{I}_{n})
), where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}_{j}=e^{2i\epsilon j}\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime}_{j}=1\quad\text{for }j=0,\ldots,n-1.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{g}-\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}|e^{2ij\epsilon}-1|^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}4|\sin(j\epsilon)|^{2}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}4(j\epsilon)^{2}={\cal O}(n^{3}\epsilon^{2})={\cal O}(n^{-2\eta})\,.

Thus,
Lemma

F.2
, we conclude

\displaystyle d_{\rm TV}\left({\cal N}(\boldsymbol{g},\mathbf{I}_{n}),{\cal N}(\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime},\mathbf{I}_{n})\right)\leq\|\boldsymbol{g}-\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime}\|_{2}={\cal O}(n^{-2\eta})\,.

Therefore, we can only distinguish the two spectral densities with probability
{\cal O}(n^{-2\eta})=o(1)
, contradicting the assumption that the algorithm succeeds with constant probability.

Next, we prove the lower bound for intensity estimation.
Let
\epsilon,\eta\in(0,1)
.
Suppose there exists an algorithm that can estimate all the locations within
\epsilon={\cal O}(n^{-0.5-\eta})
error using
n
noisy measurements with constant success probability.
Consider the spectral densities with parameters
(\mu_{1}=1,z_{1}=1)
and
(\mu_{1}^{\prime}=1+2\epsilon,z_{1}^{\prime}=1)
, respectively.
Then, the algorithm can distinguish them with constant probability since
|\mu_{1}-\mu_{1}^{\prime}|>\epsilon
.
As above, the
n
measurement outcomes are samples from
{\cal N}(\boldsymbol{1},\mathbf{I}_{n})
(or
{\cal N}((1+2\epsilon)\boldsymbol{1},\mathbf{I}_{n})
). By
Lemma

F.2
, their TV distance is at most

\displaystyle d_{\rm TV}\left({\cal N}(\boldsymbol{1},\mathbf{I}_{n}),{\cal N}((1+2\epsilon)\boldsymbol{1},\mathbf{I}_{n})\right)\leq\|\boldsymbol{1}-(1+2\epsilon)\boldsymbol{1}\|_{2}={\cal O}(n^{0.5}\epsilon)={\cal O}(n^{-\eta}),

again giving a contradiction.
∎
